**Suggested.** Treat a failed removal of the old row as success with a logged warning (the change is in force and a mount removes the leftover), or retry the removal on the next plan DDL. Put the 'newest row of a name' rule in one helper in plan_catalog (for example a ListPlans variant that marks or drops superseded rows) and use it from LoadPlans, FindPlan, the DROP TABLE RESTRICT and the sys.plans view.

**First verifier (narrowed).** [source-read] at fba5529f. The path holds as traced. src/exec/plan_catalog.cpp:781-806 (ReplaceAdminPlan): InsertDeclaration of the new row (788-793), then `admin.Install(ToAdminPlan(next), next.enabled)` (798), then DeleteDeclarationsById of the old id (799-803), whose failure is returned as the function's status. The only caller, src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:14834-14839, turns that into `Refused(altered.status())` and never reaches AwaitDdlDurability. No guard, retry or rollback of the Install sits between. The delete can fail in a live instance: DeleteDeclarationsById (src/exec/declaration_relation.cpp:264-297) walks the whole chain from desc_page_id with PageAccess::kWrite and returns any fetch, ReadTuple, RetireSlot or LogSlotRetire failure; docs/spec/page.md:142 says a page a catalog chain grows onto is not resident-class and is evictable, so a miss on a full pool (ResourceExhausted) or a read error is reachable. The retry no-op is real: FindPlan returns the highest id (plan_catalog.cpp:737-759), and command_dispatcher.cpp:14771 returns `row` with nothing written when `row.enabled == enable`, so the older row stays until a mount (LoadPlans 668-682, 728), a later DROP PLAN (which removes every row of the name, 810-815), or never by a later ALTER (that removes only the then-newest id). The sys.plans view (src/server/sys_views.cpp:992-1050) iterates ListPlans and pushes every row with its own state, with no superseded filter, whereas DROP TABLE's RESTRICT carries its own `superseded` lambda (command_dispatcher.cpp:4918-4926) and its comment names exactly this case ('an ALTER PLAN whose delete of the old row failed leaves it beside the new one'). So the three separate spellings of the newest-row rule and the view's omission are as claimed. What narrows it: (1) the title's 'durable' is not established - on this error path the new row is logged but its durability is never awaited, and if the failing step is LogSlotRetire the log is fail-stopped and the row may not survive; (2) the two-row state itself is a designed and documented state, not an unknown one: include/kds/exec/plan_catalog.hpp:104-107, docs/spec/parser-v2.md I12 ('Each change is the plan's row written again under a new id and the old row removed; a mount keeps the newest row of a name'), and docs/inflight/known-gaps.md:2627-2629 states the failure between insert and removal is not reached by any cell (state built by hand, mount handling pinned; tests/admin_plans_test.cpp:1788). None of those documents states the error reply for a change in force or the view listing the superseded row as enabled, so that residue is new.

**Second verifier (narrowed).** [source-read] at fba5529f, worktree audit-v3-4-and-architecture. I could not refute the path; I narrow the trigger and the 'durable' wording, and part of the claim is a recorded, deliberate state. Path as read. src/exec/plan_catalog.cpp:781-806 (ReplaceAdminPlan): InsertDeclaration of the new row (790-795), then admin.Install(...) at 798 under the comment 'From here the new row is the plan, whatever becomes of the old one', then DeleteDeclarationsById of the old id (799-803), whose failure is the function's return. Sole caller src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:14834-14839 turns that into Refused(altered.status()); the lambda alter_row is synchronous, with no rollback of the Install and no retry. The statement is writable from SQL (ALTER PLAN, admin, autocommit). The records are logged at kNoTxnId (declaration_relation.cpp:292), so nothing undoes the inserted row. Retry no-op is real: FindPlan answers the highest id of the name (plan_catalog.cpp:737-759) and command_dispatcher.cpp:14771 returns 'already so: nothing is written', so the client then reads ALTERED PLAN while the older row stays. A later REPLACE VARIANT removes only the then-newest id. Only a mount (LoadPlans 668-682, 728) or DROP PLAN (810-815, every row of the name) removes the leftover. The view: sys_views.cpp:992-1050 iterates exec::ListPlans, which is an unfiltered ScanDeclarations (plan_catalog.cpp:479-486), and prints each row's own flag; the superseded row passes PlanRowIsValid and PlanRowCanMatch, so it reads 'enabled' beside the new 'disabled' one. sys-views.md:113 describes the view per row and says nothing of a superseded row. The newest-row rule is spelled three times (LoadPlans, RowsNamed, DROP TABLE's lambda at command_dispatcher.cpp:4918-4926) and not in the view. What narrows it. (1) The trigger is narrower than 'an I/O error, or a full pool'. DeleteDeclarationsById (declaration_relation.cpp:264-297) fails only on a chain-page fetch, ReadTuple/RetireSlot corruption, or LogSlotRetire. FindPlan and InsertDeclaration have just walked the same chain in the same synchronous statement, and the chain's root is a resident-class page (page.md:142), so a fetch failure needs a grown sys.plans chain whose page another core's pressure evicted in that window and then a ResourceExhausted or a read error on the miss. No NoRefuseWindow or DrainOnPressure wraps plan DDL (grep of command_dispatcher.cpp: only 8457, 12235, 15178, 15291), so that refusal is admitted, but it is rare. The other trigger, a log append failure, fail-stops the log: the instance takes no further write and the restart's mount removes the leftover. (2) 'Durable' is not established: on this path AwaitDdlDurability is never reached, so the new row is logged, not awaited. (3) The two-row state is deliberate and recorded: include/kds/exec/plan_catalog.hpp:104-107, parser-v2.md I12 ('a mount keeps the newest row of a name'), the DROP TABLE comment naming exactly 'an ALTER PLAN whose delete of the old row failed', tests/admin_plans_test.cpp:1751-1791 (hand-built state, mount handling and FindPlan pinned), and known-gaps.md:2627-2629 (the failure between insert and removal is reached by no cell). None of these states the refusal reply for a change in force or the view's stale row, and neither pending audit file mentions ReplaceAdminPlan, so that residue is new. No test pins the view's output in the two-row state either way.

### H24. The reactor may block with a runnable task unpolled when a queue is longer than the loop budget

- **Kind**: logic-defect; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `sched-wait` (`sched-wait#1`)
- **Where**: `src/sched/scheduler.cpp:202`; origin: pre-3.4 (idle policy 12c0ebb1, 2026-08-26, over the loop budget of 2c6ae238); 3.4's parks (DE page-read parks, CE's parked walks, lock and commit parks) enlarge the parked population it needs
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**; second verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** IdleTimeoutMs lets the reactor block whenever the last iteration's `advanced` is false, but RunReadyTasks stops at max_tasks_per_iteration (64) polls and a task the round never reached contributes nothing to `advanced`. An iteration whose 64 polls all landed on parked tasks therefore reads as 'nothing advanced' even though tasks behind them were never asked, and the next iteration sleeps in epoll_wait with runnable work queued. sched.md section 7 says a block is permitted only after a full iteration in which nothing advanced, and the comment above this line says anything unaccounted reads as advanced; an unpolled task is unaccounted and reads as not advanced.

**Evidence.** scheduler.cpp:202 `if (HasReadyTask() && last_iteration_advanced_) return 0;` then the block of up to max_idle_block_ms (10) or the next timer. scheduler.cpp:310 `while (polled < config_.max_tasks_per_iteration) {` ends the round with `remaining[...] > 0` possible. scheduler.cpp:295 sets `advanced` only for a polled task (`result == kDone || task->advanced_in_last_poll()`), and :471-472 add only submits made this iteration before `last_iteration_advanced_ = advanced;`. A suspended task is pushed to the back of its FIFO queue (:298), so a yielding task sits behind every parked one.

**Scenario.** One core holds 64 or more parked foreground statements (65 writers queued on one hot row's lock; or streamed SELECTs parked on net.send_backpressure, of which CE allows up to 256 a core) and one walking SELECT W that yields every 64 pages. Iteration i polls W (advanced) and W goes to the back of the queue. Iteration i+1 runs with timeout 0, spends all 64 polls on parked tasks whose predicates are still false, never reaches W, and records advanced=false. Iteration i+2 blocks in PollReady until an fd event, a kick or a timer - up to 10 ms, or about 1 ms where the WAL drain timer is armed - with W runnable. W pays one idle block per slice whenever the window misses it; with 128 or more parked tasks a newly submitted statement or a just-woken lock waiter waits one block per further 64 tasks ahead of it. No wrong result: latency only, and it is hidden on a core with steady socket traffic because each fd event ends the block.

**Suggested.** Let the block wait for evidence about every queued task: allow the sleep only once the count of consecutive non-advancing polls since the last advancing event reaches the number of queued tasks (or simply carry `any remaining > 0` over as 'not yet a full iteration' and keep polling without blocking until the whole population has been asked once). Setting advanced=true whenever tasks remain would be wrong the other way: a queue of more than 64 parked tasks would then never sleep. Add a scheduler_test cell with budget+1 parked tasks and one yielding task that asserts no idle block is taken between its polls.

**First verifier (confirmed).** [source-read] in worktree audit-v3-4-and-architecture at fba5529f; nothing built or run. Traced the path end to end and found no guard. (1) src/sched/scheduler.cpp:202 `if (HasReadyTask() && last_iteration_advanced_) return 0;` is the only thing keeping the timeout at 0 while a queue is non-empty; otherwise the block is max_idle_block_ms or the next timer (:204-218). (2) RunReadyTasks (:248-317) snapshots `remaining[i] = ready_queues_[i].size()` and stops at `polled < config_.max_tasks_per_iteration` (:305, :310). The default is 64 (include/kds/sched/scheduler.hpp:57); no site in src, include or tools overrides it (grep `max_tasks_per_iteration` hits only the constructor's clamp to the group count, scheduler.cpp:22-24), and no test or sim sets it. Nothing reads `remaining[...] > 0` after the loop. (3) `advanced` is set only per polled task (:295), by an io handler (:426), a timer (:435), the post-task hook (:466) or a submit this iteration (:471); :472 stores it. A queued task the round never reached feeds none of these. (4) A parked statement stays on the ready queue: poll_one pushes a kSuspended task to the back (:298), and CoroTask::Poll answers last_poll_advanced_=false when its wait predicate is still false and nothing was resumed (include/kds/sched/coro.hpp:354-361). `Yield` (coro.hpp:435-439) arms no timer and submits nothing, so a yielded walk is just a queue entry. (5) Each in-flight statement is its own foreground CoroTask (src/server/tcp_server.cpp:732-739), all in one FIFO deque, so more than 64 concurrently parked statements on one core is the only precondition. So an iteration whose 64 polls all hit false predicates stores advanced=false with tasks behind them unasked, and the next RunOnce blocks in PollReady (:363, :389). That contradicts docs/spec/sched.md §7 (line 194-203: a block only after 'a full iteration in which nothing advanced', 'sleeps on evidence it has collected') and the comment at scheduler.cpp:200-201 ('anything unaccounted reads as advanced'). Not documented: grep of docs/inflight/known-gaps.md, docs/pending (F1-F32 and G1-G66 included), sched.md and the v3.2.0 work orders finds no statement of the budget/idle-policy interaction; tests/scheduler_test.cpp has parked_idle_blocks cells (:569, :647, :751-759) but none with more tasks than the budget. One correction to the finder's frequency, not to the defect: tasks polled after W in the same round are pushed behind W, so W does not sit behind every parked task each slice. With T queued tasks W's index moves by -64 mod T per iteration; it is polled in 64 of every T rounds. At exactly 64 parked plus W (T=65) W is skipped once per 65 slices, not every slice; about one block per slice needs roughly 128 parked, and a newly woken waiter or new statement at depth d pays up to floor(d/64) blocks. Each block is bounded by max_idle_block_ms (10 ms) and cut short by any fd event, kick or timer. Latency only, no wrong result; pre-3.4 code, with 3.4's added parks enlarging the population. Severity low for that reason.

**Second verifier (confirmed).** [source-read] worktree audit-v3-4-and-architecture at fba5529f; nothing built or run. I looked for a guard in the idle policy, the loop, the coroutine poll and the spec, and found none. Code path: src/sched/scheduler.cpp:202 is the whole test (`HasReadyTask() && last_iteration_advanced_`); HasReadyTask (:174-179) is queue-non-empty only. RunReadyTasks (:248-317) snapshots `remaining`, polls one per group then share-picks while `polled < max_tasks_per_iteration`; nothing reads leftover `remaining` after the loop. `advanced` comes only from a polled task (:295), an io handler, a timer, the post-task hook, or a submit this iteration (:471), stored at :472. max_tasks_per_iteration is 64 (include/kds/sched/scheduler.hpp:57) and grep over src, include, tests, sim finds no override beyond the constructor clamp (scheduler.cpp:22-24). A suspended task is pushed to the back of its FIFO (:298). CoroTask::Poll answers last_poll_advanced_ = false when the wait predicate is still false and nothing resumed (include/kds/sched/coro.hpp:354-361); Yield (coro.hpp:435-439) sets no wait, arms no timer and submits nothing, so a yielded walk is only a queue entry. Each in-flight statement is its own foreground CoroTask (src/server/tcp_server.cpp:732-739); lock waits and stream backpressure are WaitUntil/resume_when predicate parks (command_dispatcher.cpp:531, 775; tcp_server.cpp:724-730). I found no per-core connection cap. So an iteration whose 64 polls all hit false predicates stores advanced=false with tasks behind them unasked, and the next RunOnce blocks in PollReady. Contract: docs/spec/sched.md §7 (194-203) permits a block 'only after a full iteration in which nothing advanced ... sleeps on evidence it has collected'; §4 line 56 and invariant 4a speak of parked tasks, not of ready tasks the budget never reached. The comment at scheduler.cpp:200-201 ('anything unaccounted reads as advanced ... never a missed wake') is contradicted by an unpolled task. No spec sentence admits the budget/idle interaction as intended. Record: not in docs/inflight/known-gaps.md (idle-block entries at 124, 236, 1052, 1217, 2303, 2346 are skipped kicks, the ASH sample time and the paced gather), not in docs/pending (F/G audits), not in the v3.2.0 work orders; workorder-bl (v3.0.0):549-554 states only that a newly ready statement waits out the current iteration's polls. tests/scheduler_test.cpp has parked-block cells (542-569, 577, 603, 632, 650, 698-759) but none with more tasks than the budget, so nothing pins either behaviour. Reachability: needs 64 or more statements parked at once on one core plus a runnable one behind them. Reachable from the wire: up to 256 streamed SELECTs a core parked on a client that stopped reading (CE), or a pile of writers behind one row holder - though I note a lock wait is bounded by the 1 s fault net (command_dispatcher.cpp:442, 763), so that population is transient. Bounds on the cost, which keep it low: the drain timer is armed on every core (src/server/core_runtime.cpp:424-426, expeditor.cpp:2253) at wal_drain_interval_ns = 1 ms by default (include/kds/server/expeditor.hpp:486), and a fired timer sets advanced, so in a default server each such block is about 1 ms (rounded up to whole ms), 10 ms only with the drain interval configured 0; any fd event or kick ends it sooner. The first verifier's frequency correction is right by my own trace of the rotation: at 64 parked + 1 walker the walker is missed once in 65 iterations; about every other iteration needs roughly 128 parked. Latency only, no wrong result; the code is pre-3.4 (v3.4.0..HEAD touched scheduler.cpp twice, neither on this path).

### H25. PlanStats::Merge evicts by the previous step's seen_ns, so a posterior observed this very step can be evicted and re-created with its history lost

- **Kind**: logic-defect; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence high); round 1, `stats-stores` (`stats-stores#3`)
- **Where**: `src/stats/plan_stats.cpp:422`; origin: pre-3.4 (CC-S2 f3ced1ad, 2026-10-18)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**; second verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** When `master_.size() + fresh > kMaxMasterKeys`, `Merge` builds `by_age` from every master's `seen_ns` and erases the oldest. This runs before the loop at line 433 that stamps `master.seen_ns = now_ns` for the keys in `merged`. A key observed in this step still carries its old stamp, so it can be chosen for eviction, and ties between keys last seen in the same earlier step are broken arbitrarily by `nth_element`. The fold loop then re-creates it through `master_[key]` as an empty Master: its EWMA value resets to this step's mean, its bands are republished as unbanded (`Reband` answers moved, so `dirty_` is set), its declines and their TTL are lost, and `master_` ends the step above kMaxMasterKeys by the number of such keys. plan-stats.md:104 says the least recently seen go; a key seen this step is the most recently seen.

**Evidence.** plan_stats.cpp:425 `for (const auto& [key, master] : master_) by_age.emplace_back(master.seen_ns, key);` then :429 `for (std::size_t i = 0; i < drop; ++i) master_.erase(by_age[i].second);` and only afterwards :434-435 `Master& master = master_[key];` / `master.seen_ns = now_ns;`

**Scenario.** join_order = planned. More than 16,384 distinct planned keys are live (one join text under many namespaces is one fetch_id with many oid sets). In a steward step where 10 new keys arrive while every existing key was last stamped in the same earlier step, 10 arbitrary masters are erased, some of which ran in this step. Each of those is re-created with value = this step's mean and no hysteresis, its band may move, and `dirty_` forces a publish. The new generation re-keys every core's order memo (the cost the comment in RepublishManaged names), so join blocks are re-searched and may flip order on one noisy sample. No wrong result, only plan churn and recompiles. Under the default `fixed` a captured shape is unaffected.

**Suggested.** Stamp `seen_ns = now_ns` on the existing masters named by `merged` before building `by_age`, or exclude keys present in `merged` from the eviction candidates.

**First verifier (confirmed).** [source-read] at fba5529f in worktree audit-v3-4-and-architecture. /home/louis/ckdbs/.claude/worktrees/audit-v3-4-and-architecture/src/stats/plan_stats.cpp:419-431 builds `by_age` from every `master_` entry's `seen_ns` (line 425) with no exclusion of keys present in `merged`, and erases the `drop` smallest (line 429). The only writer of `seen_ns` in the tree is line 435, inside the fold loop that starts at 433, after the eviction (grep of src/ and include/ for `seen_ns` finds only plan_stats.hpp:224, the field, and these lines). So a master that ran in this step is ranked by its previous stamp, can be erased, and is then re-created by `master_[key]` at 434 as a default `Master` (plan_stats.hpp:218-225: present = 0, declines = 0, declined_ns = 0). With present = 0, line 444 sets value to this step's mean and `Reband` (372-380) takes the unbanded branch, so the EWMA history, the 0.75 hysteresis and the decline bits with their TTL are lost. `drop` is computed as master_.size() + fresh - kMaxMasterKeys where `fresh` (line 421) counts only keys absent before the eviction, so each evicted-then-re-created key leaves `master_` one above kMaxMasterKeys (16384, plan_stats.hpp:114) at the end of the step. I found no guard: `Merge` is called only from `StewardStep` (plan_stats.cpp:551), nothing pre-stamps, no test names kMaxMasterKeys (grep of tests, sim, fuzz empty), and plan-stats.md PS4 item 2 (docs/spec/plan-stats.md:103-105) says 'the least recently seen go', which a key seen this step is not. Not in known-gaps.md or either pending audit (grep for kMaxMasterKeys / 'least recently seen' hits only the spec line). Blame for 419-431 is f3ced1ad (2026-10-18), so pre-3.4 as the finder says. Two peripheral corrections that do not change the verdict: (1) ties are not arbitrary - `by_age` holds std::pair<seen_ns, PlanKey>, so equal stamps are ordered by key, which is deterministic but unrelated to recency; (2) the forced publish is not caused by the re-creation: line 430 sets `dirty_` on any eviction, so a new generation (and the order memo re-key via step_compiler.cpp:2891 `memo_key.generation`) happens in every evicting step anyway; what the defect adds is the reset band/declines of the re-created key in that snapshot. Reach is narrow: it needs more than 16,384 live planned keys (Fold is reached only for a chain with `chain.planned`, command_dispatcher.cpp:10591-10615) and a key among the `drop` oldest stamps running in the same step that new keys arrive. Consequence is plan churn and a lost decline fact, no wrong result. Severity low.

**Second verifier (confirmed).** [source-read] at fba5529f, worktree audit-v3-4-and-architecture. I looked for a guard and found none. Mechanism. src/stats/plan_stats.cpp:419-431: `fresh` counts merged keys absent from master_; when master_.size() + fresh > kMaxMasterKeys (16384, plan_stats.hpp:114), by_age is built from every master's seen_ns (425) with no exclusion of keys in `merged`, and the `drop` smallest are erased (429). The only write of seen_ns in the tree is line 435, inside the fold loop that begins at 433, after the eviction (grep of src/, include/, tests/, sim/ finds only the field at plan_stats.hpp:224 and these lines). So a key that ran this step is ranked by its earlier stamp, can be erased, and line 434's master_[key] then makes a default Master (present = 0): line 444 takes this step's mean as the value, Reband takes the unbanded branch (372-380), and the EWMA history, the 0.75 hysteresis and any decline bit not repeated this step are gone. master_ is an unordered_map (plan_stats.hpp:248) and nothing else erases from it, so the step ends one above the cap per such key. Contract. plan-stats.md PS4 item 2 (lines 103-105) and the code comment at 419 both say 'the least recently seen go'; a key in this step's merge is the most recently seen. No spec, work order, known-gaps entry or pending audit entry states the ordering as intended (grep for kMaxMasterKeys, 'least recently seen', 16384 in docs/pending, known-gaps.md and workorder-cc-plan-statistics.md: no hit on this). tests/plan_stats_test.cpp has no eviction cell (grep for evict, 16384, MaxMaster: empty), so nothing pins either behaviour. Reachability. Merge is called only from StewardStep (plan_stats.cpp:551), on core 0's 1 s tick (expeditor.cpp:2181). Fold is reached from RecordPlanStats (command_dispatcher.cpp:10573-10615) for any chain carrying chain.planned, which step_compiler.cpp:2765-2775 stamps 'under fixed as under planned' (CX-Q2), so the master fills under the server's default too, not only under join_order = planned as the scenario says. It needs more than 16,384 distinct {fetch_id, oids} join shapes in one run (memory-resident, never trimmed for dropped relations), then a step in which a new key arrives while a key among the oldest stamps also runs - for example the single oldest key running again in the second a new shape first appears. Narrow, but writable from plain SQL. Corrections to the claim's periphery, none changing the verdict: (a) ties are not arbitrary - by_age holds pair<seen_ns, PlanKey>, so equal stamps fall in PlanKey order, deterministic and unrelated to recency; (b) the forced publish is not caused by the re-creation, since line 430 sets dirty_ on every eviction; (c) this step's own declines are re-applied at 449-452, so only older decline bits and their TTL are lost; (d) one consequence the claim leaves out: the overshoot makes the next step evict again with fresh = 0 (size 16385 > 16384), setting dirty_ and publishing a further generation, which re-keys every core's order memo once more. Consequence stays plan-statistics churn and a lost decline fact for the affected key; under `fixed` a captured shape's order does not move. No wrong result.

### H26. A write with RETURNING whose first heading begins with "ERR " is rolled back while its reply still shows the rows and the count (text reply path only)

- **Kind**: logic-defect; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 2, `x-write-walk` (`x-write-walk#1`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:6842`; origin: EndWriteWith's reply-is-the-verdict rule predates 3.4; WithReturning's prepended heading is BU-S8 (workorder-bu-basic-query-surface.md). Not introduced in 3.4.*; not in known-gaps.md, docs/pending or round 1's list.
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**; second verifier: **not-verified**

**Claim (the finder's).** EndWriteWith decides whether a write failed by testing whether the reply text starts with "ERR ". On the text reply path WithReturning prepends the RETURNING heading, which is the first item's source text as written, to the reply before that test runs. A heading that starts with "ERR " therefore makes a successful UPDATE, DELETE or INSERT read as failed: an autocommit statement is aborted and an explicit transaction is poisoned, while the reply handed back is unchanged and still carries the returned row and "UPDATED n". KWP sessions are not affected, because their rows go to the wire sink and the reply stays "UPDATED n".

**As narrowed by verification.** On the text reply path only (debug_text_port, or in-process Dispatch with no result sink), an INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE whose first RETURNING item is an unaliased expression written starting with upper-case "ERR " (for example `RETURNING ERR + 1` over a column named ERR or err) cannot succeed. WithReturning prepends the heading (command_dispatcher.cpp:11339, 11385), and EndWriteWith's prefix test (6842) then treats the valid statement as failed: autocommit is rolled back, an explicit transaction is poisoned, and no sys.relation_stats record is made (6832). The reply is by the newline protocol's own definition (client-manual.md:541) an error reply, so engine state and reply agree for a conforming client. The defect is a spurious refusal of a valid statement whose error line is the garbled `ERR + 1\n<rows>\nUPDATED n` instead of a real message, caused by the text rendering letting a result heading collide in-band with the error prefix. It is not a silent divergence between a success reply and a rolled-back row. KWP sessions are unaffected. The verdict should come from out.status, or the heading should be joined after EndWrite.

**Evidence.** RunWalkWrite ends with FinishWrite(session, scope, WithReturning(line, session, scope, out, context), kind) (command_dispatcher.cpp:10929); HandleInsert does the same (6822-6825). WithReturning: each non-star item's heading is `item.alias.empty() ? item.text : item.alias` (11339); when the session has no result sink it sets `out.response = text_sink.Take() + "\\n" + out.response` (11385). TextResultSink::Describe writes the field names joined by ',' as the first bytes of the text (include/kds/server/result_sink.hpp:117-125). item.text is the source substring from the item's first token to the next token, trailing space trimmed (src/parser/parser.cpp:4153-4159), so `ERR + 1` is kept verbatim. FinishWrite -> EndWriteWith: `const bool failed = out.response.rfind("ERR ", 0) == 0;` then `EndWrite(session, scope, failed ? InvalidArgument(out.response) : OK)` and `if (!s.ok() && !failed) out = Refused(s)` (6838-6845), so with failed == true the reply is left as it was. EndWrite with a non-OK result: explicit transaction -> session.Poison() (13934-13939); autocommit -> AbortOwnedScope, which unwinds the trail (13946-13953, 13987-14003). FinishWrite also skips the sys.relation_stats record on the same prefix test (6832-6834). The guard would have to be at 6842-6843: the verdict should come from out.status (set by Refused at 353), not from the rendered text. There is none. WithReturning's own entry test at 11238 reads the prefix before the heading is prepended, so it does not help.

**Scenario.** Schema: CREATE TABLE t (id int64, ERR int32); INSERT INTO t VALUES (1, 5); Session on debug_text_port (or any caller of the synchronous text Dispatch), autocommit: UPDATE t SET ERR = 0 WHERE id = 1 RETURNING ERR + 1; The walk overwrites the row and returns "UPDATED 1". WithReturning builds the reply "ERR + 1\n1\nUPDATED 1". EndWriteWith sees the "ERR " prefix, EndWrite aborts the owned transaction and the overwrite is compensated. Wrong result: the reply carries the returned row and UPDATED 1, yet SELECT ERR FROM t WHERE id = 1 still answers 5. Right result: the update commits and ERR is 0, or the statement is refused with a real error line and no rows. Inside BEGIN the same statement silently poisons the transaction: the row stays written, and the next statement and COMMIT are refused. Needs the first RETURNING item to be an expression whose text as written starts with upper-case "ERR " and no alias; a bare column or `*` gives "ERR,..." or "ERR" and does not match.

**Suggested.** Take the verdict in EndWriteWith and FinishWrite from out.status (Refused already sets it; give the few bare "ERR ..." outcomes on the write paths a Status), or have WithReturning carry the returned rows in a separate field of DispatchOutcome and join them to the reply after EndWrite has run.

**First verifier (narrowed).** [source-read] at fba5529f. I tried to break the path and could not; the mechanism holds end to end. What changes is the impact. Path as read: - ParseReturning (src/parser/parser.cpp:4176) calls ParseOutputItems, which stores item.text as the raw source substring with trailing space trimmed (4153-4159). No ERR keyword exists in the parser, and column lookup is case-insensitive, so `ERR + 1` survives verbatim. - WithReturning (src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:11235) tests the prefix at 11238 before any heading exists, heads a non-star item with `item.alias.empty() ? item.text : item.alias` (11339), and with no session sink sets `out.response = text_sink.Take() + "\\n" + out.response` (11385). TextResultSink::Describe writes the names first (include/kds/server/result_sink.hpp:117-125). - Both callers pass that straight to FinishWrite (6822-6825, 10929). EndWriteWith tests `out.response.rfind("ERR ", 0) == 0` (6842) and hands EndWrite an InvalidArgument verdict; `!s.ok() && !failed` leaves the reply untouched (6844). - EndWrite: in an explicit transaction the trail has moved past the mark, so structural_rerun is false and session.Poison() runs (13934-13939). In autocommit AbortOwnedScope unwinds (13946-13953) and returns OK for an OK `reported` (14011). - FinishWrite skips the relation_stats record on the same test (6832). Guards looked for and not found: - No sanitising of the heading, no reserved-name refusal for a column called ERR, no status-based verdict on this path. HandlePurge's `ended` lambda (15082) uses the same prefix rule. - The third EndWriteWith caller (12633, SELECT ... FOR UPDATE) admits only `*` or plain columns, so it cannot produce the prefix. - KWP is unaffected: the only null-sink KWP action is the transaction frame (src/server/kwp_session.cpp:1041); portals carry a sink (700). - tests/dml_forms_test.cpp:361 shows expression items type and run (`tier * 10 AS t10`), but every expression heading tested is aliased, so no test pins this. - Not recorded in known-gaps.md, docs/pending, or workorder-bu. Why narrowed: docs/spec/client-manual.md:541 defines the newline protocol's error as any reply "always prefixed `ERR `". A conforming text client therefore reads this reply as a refusal, and the engine state (autocommit rolled back, explicit transaction failed) agrees with what that client reads. That is exactly the rule the comment at 6839-6841 states. The finder's "reply shows success, row not written" divergence exists only for a reader who ignores the prefix convention. Reach is the loopback debug_text_port (off unless configured) and in-process Dispatch callers (tests, sim).

**Second verifier (not-verified).** verifier died

## 6.3 Inefficiency the 3.4.* work added (13)

### H27. DF-S2 grew the per-core visibility slot from 64 to 72 bytes, so adjacent cores' per-statement publications now share cache lines

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `arch-concurrency` (`arch-concurrency#1`)
- **Where**: `include/kds/txn/instance_visibility.hpp:271`; origin: DF-S2 (c2925b6e), 3.4.*
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** CoreVisibilitySlot is the one per-core slot struct in the tree without alignas(64). Before DF-S2 it was four 8-byte atomics, a std::vector and a uint32, which is 64 bytes, so `std::array<CoreVisibilitySlot, kMaxCores> slots_` had a one-line stride. DF-S2 added `min_snapshot_minted_ns`, making it 72 bytes. Core k's slot now starts at 72k: core k's `ceiling_waiters` and the tail of its `open_bounds` vector sit on the line holding core k+1's `min_snapshot_lsn`, `issue_cursor`, `oldest_unresolved` and `pending_commit_bound`. Each core writes its own slot several times per statement, so neighbours now invalidate each other's lines for fields they never read (false sharing), on top of the true sharing the design already has.

**As narrowed by verification.** CoreVisibilitySlot has no alignas(64) and never had one. DF-S2 (c2925b6e) grew it from 64 to 72 bytes (by arithmetic, not compiled), so every slot now straddles two cache lines shared with a neighbour whatever the array's base alignment. Before DF-S2 that depended on where the unaligned InstanceVisibility object landed, so line-private slots were never guaranteed. The added false sharing sits on top of true sharing that already bounces every in-use slot line per mint (pending_commit_bound is read by every core and lives beside the owner's per-statement fields). The alignas plus hot/cold split is a sound suggestion; the DF-S2-specific regression is unmeasured and likely small.

**Evidence.** HEAD :271-312 has five `std::atomic<std::uint64_t>` (`min_snapshot_lsn`, `min_snapshot_minted_ns`, `issue_cursor`, `oldest_unresolved`, `pending_commit_bound`), `std::vector<std::uint64_t> open_bounds`, and `std::atomic<std::uint32_t> ceiling_waiters`; `git show c2925b6e^:include/kds/txn/instance_visibility.hpp` has the same struct without `min_snapshot_minted_ns`. `grep alignas` finds `struct alignas(64) Slot` in sched/core_load.hpp:50, server/statement_epoch.hpp:122, txn/holder_progress.hpp:49 and base/contention.hpp:178, and none in instance_visibility.hpp. Sizes are by arithmetic for x86-64 libstdc++, not compiled.

**Suggested.** Make the slot `alignas(64)` and split it in two lines: what the owner writes per statement (`min_snapshot_lsn`, `min_snapshot_minted_ns`, `oldest_unresolved`, `issue_cursor`) apart from what every other core reads per mint and per commit (`pending_commit_bound`, `ceiling_waiters`). Add a `static_assert` on the size. Measure in build-release with the `point` and `insert1` census cells at cores 2 and 4, the cells of docs/pending/multicore-scaling-cores4-below-cores2.md.

### H28. C_EXECUTE copies the bound statement text out of a portal that can never be executed again

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence high); round 1, `arch-hot-path` (`arch-hot-path#4`)
- **Where**: `src/server/kwp_session.cpp:699`; origin: pre-3.4 (eecda943, 2026-08-31)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** OnBind builds the substituted text and moves it into `portal.sql` (608); OnExecute then copies it into the FrameAction (`action.sql = portal->sql`), and tcp_server.cpp:668 moves that copy into `conn.current_line` precisely so the text outlives the portal. A grep of src/server and include/kds/server shows line 699 is the only read of a portal's `sql`, and OnExecute refuses a second execution of the same portal (673-680, 'has already been executed'), so the original string is dead the moment it is copied. Every KWP statement therefore pays one extra heap allocation and a memcpy of the whole statement text between BIND and the dispatch, on top of the substitution's own allocation.

**Evidence.** kwp_session.cpp:699 `action.sql = portal->sql;` kwp_session.cpp:673-680 `if (portal->executed) { ... "' has already been executed; use C_CONTINUE for more rows, or C_BIND again to run it afresh"` tcp_server.cpp:668 `conn.current_line = std::move(action.sql);`

**Suggested.** `action.sql = std::move(portal->sql);` (the portal keeps `statement`, its sink and its flags, which are all C_CONTINUE and C_DESCRIBE read). Visible only as allocation count per statement; fold it into whichever A/B the first finding gets.

### H29. A statement reads the wall clock twice and the access-statistics record reads the monotonic clock again, where the head's instants already exist - the remedy DF applied to the index-use fold was not applied here

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `arch-hot-path` (`arch-hot-path#5`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:1469`; origin: RecordStatement's wall-clock read: DF-S1 (44dddd27, 2026-11-18, 3.4.13); RecordAccessShapes' clock read: pre-3.4, carried through DA-S1 (89c26252)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** Three per-statement clock reads duplicate an instant the statement already holds, and none is in DF's recorded spike list (which names the compile clock and the session cost sample only). (1) RecordStatement calls exec::StatementContextNow() - std::chrono::system_clock::now() (functions.cpp:106) - only to stamp first_seen/last_seen and the refusal row, although the same statement took a wall instant at its head through StatementContextFor (HandleSelect 10015, HandleInsert 6820, RunWalkWrite 10886), itself taken whether or not the statement uses NOW(). (2) RecordAccessShapes (10627) passes a fresh NowNs() as last_seen for every successful SELECT with access_statistics on (the default, expeditor.hpp:306), while RecordIndexUse eleven lines above (10568) uses statement_head_ns_ and its comment claims 'no latch, no lookup, no clock' - known-gaps.md records that DF removed that fold's clock read. (3) DispatchAsync's began_ns (952) and StageStatement's statement_head_ns_ (1270) are two monotonic reads microseconds apart for the first run of a statement.

**Evidence.** command_dispatcher.cpp:1469 `const std::int64_t now_us = exec::StatementContextNow().now_us;` command_dispatcher.cpp:10627 `stats::RecordChainAccess(*access_stats_, core_id_, chain, static_cast<std::uint64_t>(NowNs()));` command_dispatcher.cpp:10568 `static_cast<std::uint64_t>(statement_head_ns_));`

**Suggested.** Take one wall instant per statement (in StageStatement, or derive it from the monotonic head with a per-core anchor) and hand it to both the StatementContext and RecordStatement; stamp access statistics with the statement's head instant. Carry that instant on the SelectRun rather than reading the member: known-gaps.md already notes that a statement resumed after a park sees statement_head_ns_ of whichever statement its core staged last, and Finish runs after a sliced walk. The gain is a few vDSO clock reads per statement, to be measured as server CPU on the point-SELECT cell; it is below what one server lifetime resolves on this host, so it should ride a pooled interleaved A/B, not its own run.

### H30. ManagedStatistics::forgotten_ gains one node for every committed DROP TABLE and is never trimmed

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence high); round 1, `arch-state-stores` (`arch-state-stores#4`)
- **Where**: `src/stats/managed_statistics.cpp:159`; origin: CY-S1b (61f5dddd)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** Forget inserts the oid into `forgotten_` unconditionally, before it looks for a slot, and the dispatcher calls it for every committed drop whether or not the relation ever had a statistics object. Nothing erases from the set, so it grows for the life of the instance with the number of tables dropped, and Store and Lock search it under the store's one latch. Its only purpose is to fence a GATHER that was in flight across the drop.

**Evidence.** managed_statistics.cpp:157-160: `const LatchGuard hold(&latch_); forgotten_.insert(oid); const auto it = slots_.find(oid); if (it == slots_.end()) return false;`. command_dispatcher.cpp:3751: `if (plan_stats_->managed().Forget(oid)) plan_stats_->RepublishManaged();`, reached from 5001 and 9169 for every drop. The only other uses are the two lookups at :56 and :126. No hit for `forgotten_` in known-gaps, plan-stats.md or the CY work order.

**Suggested.** Fence only what can race: have a gather register its oid while it runs and let Forget mark only registered oids, or have the gather's publish re-resolve the relation under its last slice's IS. Either removes the set.

### H31. Every DispatchAsync statement allocates an AwaitStatementWaits coroutine frame even with nothing to wait for; the comment above the call says the opposite

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `dispatcher-1` (`dispatcher-1#2`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:1015`; origin: pre-3.4 (the unconditional call dates from BA-S14 6aa76ec7 / AO-S6e-a); 3.4's DE and A1 stage 2 added arms inside it
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** DispatchAsync calls `co_await AwaitStatementWaits(...)` unconditionally after every dispatch (1015, and again per re-run at 1038 and 1080). AwaitStatementWaits is a sched::Coro function, Coro::promise_type has no operator new (include/kds/sched/coro.hpp:72-124), and AwaitCoro::await_ready is always false and links the child's handle into the parent's promise, so the frame is a heap allocation plus a suspend/resume hop through CoroTask::Poll's DeepestPending loop on every statement. For the common statement (an INSERT/UPDATE that met no blocker, a point SELECT run inline by RunInline) the body tests three optionals, parked_write, select_run and build_run, and returns. The comment at 1007-1010 states a property the code does not have.

**As narrowed by verification.** DispatchAsync calls the Coro function AwaitStatementWaits unconditionally after every dispatch (command_dispatcher.cpp:1015, 1038, 1080), so every statement creates one more coroutine frame and one extra resume inside the same Poll, even when no wait, select_run or build_run is pending; whether the toolchain elides the allocation is not established (the child handle escapes into the parent's promise, so elision is unlikely), and the cost is unmeasured. The comment at 1007-1010 is not the opposite of the code: it was written about AwaitWriteBlock's frame (f48d2139 shows the sentence predates the wrapper and described AwaitWriteBlock directly), which is still created only where out->write_block has a value (664-667); the sentence is merely ambiguous now that the wrapper stands between.

**Evidence.** 1009-1010: `// produced. The frame is allocated only where there is something to` / `// wait for.`; 1015: `co_await AwaitStatementWaits(line, session, out, &statement_deadline_ns);`; coro.hpp:247: `bool await_ready() const noexcept { return false; }`

**Suggested.** Guard the call with a plain predicate at the three sites - `out->page_wait || out->write_block || out->lock_wait || out->select_run || out->build_run || session.parked_write()` - so the frame exists only where a wait or a handed-on run does, which is what the comment already claims. Measure in build-release as server CPU per statement on a resident point SELECT and a single-row INSERT, interleaved A/B; I claim no number, and whether the default toolchain elides the frame was not checked by building.

### H32. Since DE-S1 a one-core instance writes load words on every statement and every connect, for a table nothing reads

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence high); round 1, `expeditor-session` (`expeditor-session#2`)
- **Where**: `src/server/tcp_server.cpp:671`; origin: DE-S1 (0d3ee8fe)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** DE made core 0 attach a wake table at cores = 1 (for the page reader), which makes Scheduler::wake_armed() true there. DE's review stopped the scheduler publishing a load word at one core (publish_load = false), but the listener's two load-slot sites still gate on wake_armed(). So at cores = 1 every KWP statement now bumps CoreLoad heads or inside, loads window_end_ns and compares the session's counted window, and every accept and close rewrites sessions. CoreLoad::SetCores(0) means SHOW META prints no load block and no placer exists, so the words have no reader. This is against BL-R9 (core_load.hpp:28 'Written only above one core') and against CountSession's own comment.

**Evidence.** expeditor.cpp:1860-1865: `if (config_.cores == 1) { page_read_wakers_.emplace(1U); scheduler.AttachWakerTable(&*page_read_wakers_, /*core_id=*/0, /*publish_load=*/false);` tcp_server.cpp:671-673: `if (scheduler_->wake_armed()) { ... CoreLoad::Bump(head ? load.heads : load.inside);` and tcp_server.cpp:437 `if (!scheduler_->wake_armed()) return;` under the comment 'At one core there is no other core to read it (BL-R9)'. scheduler.hpp:465: `bool wake_armed() const noexcept { return waker_.has_value(); }`; workorder-de line 702 records the same fix applied to the scheduler only.

**Suggested.** Gate both listener sites on the scheduler's publishes_load_ (expose it as an accessor) instead of wake_armed(), the predicate RecordIterationHold already uses.

### H33. The session wait registry is one instance-wide mutex on a session's first statement and its close, held by a view for a copy of every session

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence high); round 1, `sched-wait` (`sched-wait#2`)
- **Where**: `src/sched/wait_state.cpp:52`; origin: CJ WE05-WE06 (db2696fe)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** MakeSessionWaitStats and its deleter take the process-wide `g_sessions_mu`, and SnapshotSessionWaits holds the same std::mutex while it allocates a vector and copies every live session's SessionWaitStats (about 448 bytes each: 21 events x 16 bytes plus 13 words). A session's first statement on any core (TcpServer::SubmitStatement calls conn.session.WaitStats(), which creates lazily) and every connection close on a reactor therefore block that core's reactor thread for as long as a `sys.session_state` or `sys.session_waits` read on another core is copying, and contend with each other across cores under connection churn. This is what rules.md section 3a clauses (a), (b) and (c) forbid (an instance-wide latch on a statement's path, a record waiting for a view's builder, an allocation and an O(sessions) scan under the hold). known-gaps.md's DL section does not list it and states the opposite: 'What already meets the rule: ... the wait statistics'. The DL work order's site table has AshRing but not this registry.

**Evidence.** wait_state.cpp:53-56 `std::lock_guard<std::mutex> guard(g_sessions_mu); ... out.reserve(g_sessions.size()); for (const SessionWaitStats* s : g_sessions) out.push_back(*s);`. wait_state.cpp:37-38 the deleter: `std::lock_guard<std::mutex> guard(g_sessions_mu); std::erase(g_sessions, gone);` - a linear erase over every session, under the mutex. tcp_server.cpp:738 `task->wait_state_mut().session = conn.session.WaitStats();` and session.hpp:129 `if (wait_stats_ == nullptr) wait_stats_ = sched::MakeSessionWaitStats();`.

**Suggested.** Keep the registry per core (a session registers in the part of the core it is created on, a view merges the parts) or make registration a lock-free push onto a per-core list that the view reads without the writer's latch; at minimum copy the pointer list under the mutex and the 448-byte bodies outside it, and replace the deleter's linear std::erase with an index or an intrusive link. Add the site to known-gaps' DL list and to the DL work order's table so DL-S0's census covers it, and correct the 'wait statistics already meet the rule' line.

### H34. WaitOnPollEnter reads the clock before the scoped early return and discards the reading

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence high); round 1, `sched-wait` (`sched-wait#3`)
- **Where**: `src/sched/wait_state.cpp:241`; origin: CJ WE02 (the hooks in CoroTask::Poll; CJ milestone)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** Every poll that resumes a task parked inside a WaitScope - the resume after a commit's WAL-flush park, a lock wait, a ceiling wait, a DE page-read park, a backpressure park - reads the wait clock in WaitOnPollEnter and then returns without using it, because nothing of a scoped wait ends at the poll's entry. The scope's own destructor reads the clock again a few instructions later. The CJ work order records the general cost ('four clock reads per resuming poll instead of two', unifying the two clocks left open); this one read is separate from that seam and is removable without touching it.

**Evidence.** wait_state.cpp:240-242 `c.current = &task;` / `const MonoTimeNs now = WaitNow();` / `if (task.scoped) return;  // still inside its scope: nothing of the wait ends here`. WaitNow is an out-of-line call that loads the thread's clock pointer and makes a virtual Clock::Now (steady_clock through the vDSO).

**Suggested.** Move `const MonoTimeNs now = WaitNow();` below the `if (task.scoped) return;` line. It would show, if anywhere, in the CJ P1 cell's commit-heavy arm (one park and one resume per commit) against the KDS_WAIT_INSTRUMENTATION_OFF build; no number is claimed.

### H35. Per-core statistics parts share cache lines: AccessStats' slots_ sits beside core 0's latch, and adjacent cores' parts are packed unaligned

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `stats-stores` (`stats-stores#1`)
- **Where**: `include/kds/stats/access_stats.hpp:138`; origin: DA (the flat-table AccessStats, v3.4.15) for access_stats.hpp; DF-S3 83aad532 for oid_parts.hpp; BO (pre-3.4) for statement_stats.hpp and per_core_ring.hpp
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** The stores' headers promise that a record writes its own core's part and nothing shared (DA-R4; statement_stats.hpp:44 'No cross-core write is added to a statement's path'). That holds for words but not for cache lines. Every part is an element of one contiguous `std::array<Part, kStatsParts>` with no `alignas`, and each record locks and unlocks a `std::mutex` that is the part's first member. (1) In AccessStats, `const std::size_t slots_` is declared immediately before `parts_`, so it lies 8 bytes before `parts_[0].latch`. `Record` on every core reads `slots_` (HomeOf's modulus, the wrap test, the load bound) once or more per step, while core 0 writes its mutex twice per step it records. Unless a line boundary falls exactly between the two members, every core's record reads a line core 0 keeps dirtying. (2) An AccessStats part is 80 bytes (Latch 48 = std::mutex 40 + kind, table pointer, live, dropped, failed), so part k's `table` pointer, read on every record, shares a line with part k+1's mutex for about half the adjacent pairs. (3) A StatementStats or OidParts part is 112 bytes (Latch 48, unordered_map 56, dropped 8), so part k's map header words (`_M_before_begin`, `_M_element_count`, read by every `find`) share a line with part k+1's mutex for some pairs (parts 0/1, 4/5, ... when the array is line-aligned). Above one core, each statement end therefore takes cache-line transfers the design meant to exclude: once per statement for StatementStats, once per chain step for AccessStats, since `RecordChainAccess` takes the latch per step. Sizes are derived from the source assuming glibc x86-64 (sizeof(std::mutex) == 40); nothing was compiled or measured.

**As narrowed by verification.** The per-core statistics parts (AccessStats, OidParts, StatementStats, PerCoreRing) are contiguous, unaligned arrays of latch-first parts, and AccessStats' slots_ sits 8 bytes before core 0's mutex, so the header promise of 'nothing shared' holds for words and not for cache lines; RecordChainAccess also takes the part latch once per step rather than once per chain. The cost is unmeasured and, for AccessStats on a point SELECT at cores = 8, bounded by DA's close A/B at about 0.4 us a statement (statistics on against off, inside the spread). It is a layout item for DL to fix while it rewrites these record paths, not a shown regression.

**Evidence.** access_stats.hpp:138-139 `const std::size_t slots_;` / `mutable std::array<Part, kStatsParts> parts_;`; access_stats.cpp:62 `for (std::size_t i = HomeOf(kind, rel_id, column_mask, slots_);;`; oid_parts.hpp:91-96 and statement_stats.hpp:173-178 `struct Part { Latch latch{LatchKind::kStatsRing}; std::unordered_map<...>; std::uint64_t dropped = 0; }; ... std::array<Part, kStatsParts>`. A grep for alignas / cache line / false sharing over include/kds/stats, src/stats, known-gaps.md, docs/pending and the DA/DF/DI/DL work orders finds nothing about these stores (DL line 216 is about the contention instrument only). PlanStats already allocates each part separately (`std::vector<std::unique_ptr<Part>>`).

**Suggested.** Give each `Part` `alignas(64)` (or allocate parts separately, as PlanStats does) in AccessStats, OidParts, StatementStats and PerCoreRing, and move `slots_` off the line of `parts_[0]` (or copy it into each part). Take the AccessStats part latch once per chain in `RecordChainAccess` instead of once per step. Measure in build-release at cores = 8 with the interleaved A/B point SELECT and join SELECT cells DA's results files use (server CPU per statement). DL's strict end state (no latch) still needs the alignment, because single-writer words packed across parts share lines the same way.

### H36. DB's owed-image test adds a third structure-latch acquisition and a second frame-table lookup to every write fetch, owed or not

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `storage-pool-delta` (`storage-pool-delta#1`)
- **Where**: `src/storage/device_page_store.cpp:2510`; origin: DB-S1 (c802fac9)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** Since DB, once images are enabled (expeditor.cpp:1315), every FetchPinned for write calls LogOwedImage, which takes the instance's one structure latch and does frames_.find(page_id) just to read Frame::owes_image. This happens even when nothing is owed, which is every write fetch of a page after its first one since a writeback copy. The same fetch has already taken that latch twice with the frame in hand: once in FetchAndPin (2638-2657) and once in AcquirePageLatch's post-grant dirty mark (2966-2969), which runs after the exclusive latch is granted, the exact point the header says the debt must be decided at. CreatePinned adds a further separate hold just to clear the bit (2798-2799), immediately after AcquirePageLatch released its own. On an armed pool (cores > 1) a write fetch therefore goes from 3 to 4 structure-latch acquisitions (fetch, mark, owed test, unpin) on the latch every core's fetch and unpin share; at cores = 1 the latch is a no-op and the cost is the extra hash lookup.

**Evidence.** device_page_store.cpp:2510-2513: `LatchGuard structure(structure_latch()); auto it = frames_.find(page_id); if (it == frames_.end() || !it->second.owes_image) return Status::OK();` device_page_store.cpp:2966-2968: `if (mode == PinMode::kExclusive) { LatchGuard structure(structure_latch()); frame.MarkDirty(++dirty_gens_);` device_page_store.cpp:2798-2799: `LatchGuard structure(latch); frame->owes_image = false;`

**Suggested.** Read and clear owes_image inside the hold AcquirePageLatch already takes for an exclusive grant (frame reference in hand, no lookup, already after the grant), and hand the answer to FetchPinned. At cores = 1 take it from the frame Resolve found. LogOwedImage then touches the latch only when an image is really owed (to restore the bit on a refused append, and through StampPageLsn). CreatePinned's clear folds into the same hold. Measure in build-release as server CPU per write statement, interleaved A/B, at cores = 1 and at 4 or more cores on a resident update or insert workload; the multi-core case is where the shared latch should show. I did not establish whether DB's or DH's close measurement priced this component separately; they report the image's volume cost.

### H37. LogFullPageImage's write fetch can log a write-fetch image immediately before the whole-page image of the same bytes

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `storage-pool-delta` (`storage-pool-delta#2`)
- **Where**: `include/kds/storage/log_page_image.hpp:35`; origin: DB-S1 (c802fac9) made Get an image site; LogFullPageImage itself is pre-3.4
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** LogFullPageImage reads the page through store.Get, a write fetch, so since DB it runs LogOwedImage first. If the frame owes an image (a writeback copy fell between the mutation's own fetch and this call, or the page was just faulted back), the store appends a PACKED_PAGE_IMAGE or FULL_PAGE_IMAGE of the page as it stands, and LogFullPageImage then appends a second, verbatim 8,192-byte FULL_PAGE_IMAGE of the same bytes under the same exclusive hold, with no record between them. The first image heals nothing the second does not: redo's image arm creates and fills the page from the later one. Logged-class callers are wal_row_log.cpp:50 and :94 (var-heap and heap link edits), catalog.cpp:357 (LogCatPageImage, a chain's old tail) and assertion_build.cpp:170. The fenced index build's calls are exempt because its owner is registered unlogged. The same redundancy holds where a write fetch's image is followed directly by a BTREE_SPLIT whole image of that page. How often the debt is outstanding at these sites was not established; known-gaps records only that these whole pages are not packed (DH-Q13), not the back-to-back pair.

**As narrowed by verification.** The redundant image is logged by the caller's outer write fetch, not by LogFullPageImage's nested Get. When a chain growth's link edit is the first write fetch of the old tail since its fault-in or last writeback copy, that fetch logs an owed image of the pre-edit page (packed, or verbatim if it does not pack), and the FULL_PAGE_IMAGE of the post-edit page follows with no record of that page between them, so the earlier image heals nothing the later one does not. Sites: catalog.cpp:564-566, assertion_build.cpp:165-170, and the var-heap and heap chain growths logged by wal_row_log.cpp:50 and :94. The same holds for a write fetch's image followed directly by a BTREE_SPLIT image of that page. It is bounded at one extra image per page per copy, and only when the link edit rather than an earlier append is the first write after the copy; the frequency was not established. known-gaps.md ('Only the write fetch's image is packed', DH-Q13 (a)) records only that the whole-page records are verbatim and unmeasured, not this pair.

**Evidence.** log_page_image.hpp:35: `auto bytes = store.Get(page_id);` then :39 `wal->Append(wal::RecordSpec{wal::RecordType::kFullPageImage, txn_id, page_id}, bytes.value().bytes())` device_page_store.cpp:2491-2496: `auto bytes = FetchAndPin(...); if (!bytes.ok() || for_read) return bytes; ... if (Status s = LogOwedImage(page_id, bytes.value()); !s.ok())`

**Suggested.** Give the store a write fetch that declares the caller is about to log the whole page (or let LogFullPageImage clear the debt and log its own image as the owed one), so an owed page logs one image. Count before building: pool_fetch_images against FULL_PAGE_IMAGE records per page on a spill-heavy insert workload and a chain-growth workload, across a checkpoint cadence.

### H38. CoreVisibilitySlot is unaligned and DF-S2 grew it from 64 to 72 bytes: every core's per-statement publication now dirties lines its neighbour and every minting core read

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `txn-locks-delta` (`txn-locks-delta#1`)
- **Where**: `include/kds/txn/instance_visibility.hpp:271`; origin: DF-S2 (c2925b6e) added the fifth atomic; the missing alignment is pre-3.4
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** The per-core visibility slots are a plain std::array<CoreVisibilitySlot, kMaxCores> with no alignas. Before DF-S2 a slot was 4 atomics (32) + a vector (24) + a u32 (padded to 8) = 64 bytes; DF-S2's min_snapshot_minted_ns makes it 72, so consecutive cores' slots necessarily straddle cache lines. The owning core stores into its slot several times per statement, a point autocommit SELECT included (RegisterReader and UnregisterReader each run PublishCoreBounds: a seq_cst store of min_snapshot_lsn, the new relaxed stamp store, an exchange on oldest_unresolved, a cursor store). Every mint on every other core reads each in-use slot's pending_commit_bound (SnapshotCeiling, called twice per MintReadView: manager.cpp:120 and :94), and HorizonLsn/PendingCommitBound walk the same slots. So above one core each statement head takes line transfers caused by other cores' reader-lease publications, and a core's own stores invalidate part of its neighbour's slot. Every other per-core slot in the tree is alignas(64) (ContentionSlot, CoreLoad::Slot, HolderProgress::Slot, StatementEpochs::Slot). DF's close states that cores above 1 did not resolve, so this cost was not measured.

**As narrowed by verification.** CoreVisibilitySlot has no alignas and is 72 bytes since DF-S2 (64 before), unlike every other per-core slot in the tree. Within one slot the field every other core's mint reads twice (pending_commit_bound, offset 32) shares a line with the fields the owner rewrites per statement (min_snapshot_lsn at 0, the stamp at 8, oldest_unresolved at 24); that intra-slot sharing predates 3.4. DF-S2 added one relaxed store to that already-dirtied line and made neighbour slots necessarily straddle lines. The issue cursor is not stored on the reader-lease paths. The cost above one core is unmeasured (DF's close did not resolve cores = 8) and no document records the layout as a decision.

**Evidence.** instance_visibility.hpp:271-283 `struct CoreVisibilitySlot { std::atomic<std::uint64_t> min_snapshot_lsn{...}; ... std::atomic<std::uint64_t> min_snapshot_minted_ns{kNoMintStamp};` (no alignas); :661 `std::array<CoreVisibilitySlot, server::kMaxCores> slots_{};`; instance_visibility.cpp:156-159 `for (core...) { const std::uint64_t bound = slots_[core].pending_commit_bound.load();`. `git show c2925b6e^:include/kds/txn/instance_visibility.hpp` shows the four-atomic 64-byte slot.

**Suggested.** Make the slot alignas(64) and split it by who touches what: the fields other cores read at every mint (pending_commit_bound) on one line, the fields the owner rewrites per statement (min_snapshot_lsn, the stamp, oldest_unresolved, issue_cursor) on another, the owner-only open_bounds/ceiling_waiters apart. Measure where DF's close could not: server CPU per point SELECT and per point UPDATE at cores = 2, 4, 8 in build-release, interleaved A/B.

### H39. Every write fetch takes the pool's structure latch a second time, and looks the frame up again, to test owes_image

- **Kind**: inefficiency; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `wal-delta` (`wal-delta#1`)
- **Where**: `src/storage/device_page_store.cpp:2507`; origin: DB-S1 (c802fac9, 2026-11-19)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** LogOwedImage runs on every FetchPinned for write. Before it knows whether an image is owed, it takes the instance's one frames latch and does frames_.find(page_id). The exclusive pin a few lines earlier has already taken that same latch with the Frame in hand to mark it dirty (line 2966-2969). So DB added one more acquisition of the instance-wide structure latch and one more hash lookup to every write fetch at cores > 1, including the common case where the page owes nothing.

**Evidence.** device_page_store.cpp:2966-2968: `if (mode == PinMode::kExclusive) { LatchGuard structure(structure_latch()); frame.MarkDirty(++dirty_gens_);` and its comment "its cost is one structure-latch hold per exclusive fetch". Then :2510-2512 in LogOwedImage: `LatchGuard structure(structure_latch()); auto it = frames_.find(page_id); if (it == frames_.end() || !it->second.owes_image) return Status::OK();`. structure_latch() is the single frames_latch_ (device_page_store.hpp:1639). The DB and DH work orders describe the bit's clear under the structure latch as a fact, but do not name the second hold as a cost; grep of known-gaps.md and both audit files found nothing.

**Suggested.** Read and clear owes_image inside the exclusive pin's existing hold at line 2967 (same latch, same frame, page latch already exclusive) and hand the answer to FetchPinned, so LogOwedImage is entered only when an image is owed. The failure path that re-sets the bit stays as it is. Measure in build-release at cores > 1 on a write-heavy cell (server CPU per UPDATE, interleaved A/B) and on the frames latch's wait tallies.

## 6.4 Performance opportunities (21)

### H40. Every statement's view mint walks every core's slot twice, and every reader register/unregister rewrites its own slot unconditionally, so slot lines bounce per statement

- **Kind**: performance; severity medium (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `arch-concurrency` (`arch-concurrency#2`)
- **Where**: `src/txn/manager.cpp:123`; origin: pre-3.4 (AN/BP); DF-S2 added one more store per publication
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** An autocommit read costs, on shared lines: (1) `MintReadView` calls `SnapshotCeiling()` twice (manager.cpp:123 for `LowerSnapshotBound`'s argument, then :94 in `MintCheckView`), and each call loads `slots_in_use_` and every in-use core's `pending_commit_bound`; (2) `RegisterReader` and `UnregisterReader` each run `PublishCoreBounds` (manager.cpp:779, :796), which does a seq_cst store of `min_snapshot_lsn`, a store of `min_snapshot_minted_ns`, and an unconditional `oldest_unresolved.exchange` (instance_visibility.cpp:45) that takes the line exclusive even when a read-only statement leaves the value at `kUnboundedBound`. `pending_commit_bound`, which every other core's mint reads, is in the same struct as the fields the owner rewrites per statement. So each statement on core A misses on every other core's slot line, and each of A's publications must re-take a line its peers just pulled to shared. That cost grows with the core count on a read-only workload that touches no latch at all. `EndCommit` (instance_visibility.cpp:121) also loads every core's `ceiling_waiters` per commit. This is a candidate mechanism for the `point` cell's k4/k2 = 0.79; the pending note lists only latch spins, drains, carves, checkpoints and skipped kicks.

**As narrowed by verification.** Per autocommit read, the owning core writes its CoreVisibilitySlot about seven times (LowerSnapshotBound; then at register and again at unregister an unconditional seq_cst min_snapshot_lsn store, a minted_ns store and an unconditional oldest_unresolved exchange) and walks every in-use core's pending_commit_bound twice. Those lines are read by every other core's mint, so slot lines bounce per statement with no latch involved, and the cost grows with the core count. Suggestion (b), compare before store in PublishSnapshotBound and PublishOldestUnresolved, and the line split are available. Suggestion (a), one ceiling read per mint, is not: the second read must follow the lowering store (AN-8 C1, manager.cpp:110-122). Whether this explains the cores-4 result is unmeasured.

**Evidence.** manager.cpp:123 `visibility_->LowerSnapshotBound(core_, visibility_->SnapshotCeiling());` followed by `return MintCheckView(own_trx_id);`, whose :94 is `view.snapshot_lsn = visibility_->SnapshotCeiling();`. instance_visibility.cpp:156 `const std::uint64_t bound = slots_[core].pending_commit_bound.load();` inside the `in_use` loop. instance_visibility.cpp:45 `slots_[core].oldest_unresolved.exchange(trx_id, std::memory_order_release);` with no compare first. That the dispatcher registers a reader for every autocommit SELECT is taken from `MintReadView`'s own comment ("inside `RegisterReader` for an autocommit statement"), not traced.

**Suggested.** (a) Take the ceiling once per mint and pass it to both uses, if AN-8 C1's ordering argument allows the single read to stand for both. (b) In `PublishOldestUnresolved` and `PublishSnapshotBound`, load and compare before storing, so an unchanged value writes nothing; the slot has one writer, so load-compare-store is sound, as `LowerSnapshotBound` already relies on. (c) Separate the lines as in the previous finding. Attribute with `perf c2c` or the sampler on a read-only `point` cell at cores 2 against 4.

### H41. Releasing a row's unique-key units scans the transaction's whole lock ledger, once per row written

- **Kind**: performance; severity medium (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `dispatcher-4` (`dispatcher-4#1`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:13710`; origin: pre-3.4 (BX-S2, e542bb7d, 2026-10-15)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** UniqueKeyHolds::Release gives back a row's one or two unique-key units through LockTable::ReleaseIf, which is one pass over every entry the transaction holds, with a std::find over the units per entry. It runs once per row that carries or moves a unique key: after MaintainIndexes in UpdateParsed (:12365) and in the destructor of InsertOneRow's holds (:8350) and of the ON CONFLICT arbiter's (:8066). Because row units are held to the decide, the ledger grows by one entry per row, so a statement or transaction that writes N such rows does on the order of N squared / 2 ledger visits, up to the 65,536-entry cap of max_locks_per_txn.

**Evidence.** command_dispatcher.cpp:13712-13713 `locks_->ReleaseIf(txn_->id(), txn_->borrows(), [&](const txn::LockHoldings::Held& h) { return std::find(units_.begin(), units_.end(), h.key) != units_.end();`. lock_table.hpp:648-656 documents ReleaseIf as 'one pass over the ledger: O(ledger) however many go' and implements it as remove_if over holdings.held_ (a std::vector, :578). WriteWalk::Claim (:11631-11633) adds a Tuple unit per qualifying row when no window is declared; InsertOneRow's named-key arm adds LockKey::Tuple(ta.oid, supplied_id) (:8462-8470).

**Scenario.** Not a wrong result. `UPDATE t SET email = email || 'x' WHERE status = 1` on a relation with a unique index on email, matching 50,000 rows: no tuple-key window is declared, so each row takes its U unit and each row's Release scans every unit taken so far, on the reactor, in a walk that does not yield. The same shape for an explicit transaction of many named-key INSERTs into a unique-indexed relation.

**Suggested.** The key units are the newest entries of the ledger when Release runs (taken after the row's own unit, nothing taken between the check and the release), so search and erase from the ledger's tail - a ReleaseNewest(txn, holdings, units) that walks back at most units.size() plus a small slack - or have BorrowChain hand back the ledger index for UniqueKeyHolds to keep. Measure in build-release with a multi-row UPDATE that moves a unique key under a non-key WHERE, and with a bulk named-key INSERT in one transaction into a unique-indexed relation, at 1,000 / 10,000 / 60,000 rows: server CPU per row should stop growing with the row count.

### H42. Assertion mount recovery restores groups in O(groups^2): RestoreGroup scans every group for each one restored

- **Kind**: performance; severity medium (the finder's: medium, confidence high); round 1, `exec-cabin-transition` (`exec-cabin-transition#2`)
- **Where**: `src/exec/bound_cabin.cpp:214`; origin: pre-3.4 (db5fe2d3, RC07 AS6a group ids, 2026-08-12)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** BoundCabin::RestoreGroup calls FindById, a linear scan over every bucket of groups_by_hash_, once per group, and assertion recovery calls RestoreGroup once per snapshot group. Restoring G groups is therefore about G^2/2 header visits at every mount. AdoptGroupId (line 201) pays the same scan for each group first created during redo. AttachEntries, a few lines below, already builds an id-to-header map to avoid exactly this cost for entries, but nothing does it for groups.

**Evidence.** bound_cabin.cpp:170-176 FindById loops `for (auto& [hash, bucket] : groups_by_hash_)` over every header. bound_cabin.cpp:214 `if (FindById(group_id) != nullptr) {`. assertion_recover.cpp:200-201 `for (const PendingGroup& group : run.groups) { ... a->cabin->RestoreGroup(group.group_id, group.key, group.count,`.

**Suggested.** Keep a group_id index beside groups_by_hash_ (an unordered_map<uint32_t, GroupHeader*>, or a dense vector since ids are dense from 1) and have FindById, RestoreGroup, AdoptGroupId and AttachEntry use it. The gain is at mount and restore for a GROUP BY assertion with many groups (for example one per account); measure mount time against group count in build-release.

### H43. Reply path: one heap allocation per row and about seven copies of every row byte between encode and send()

- **Kind**: performance; severity medium (the finder's: medium, confidence high); round 1, `net-wire` (`net-wire#3`)
- **Where**: `src/server/kwp_session.cpp:235`; origin: pre-3.4 (KW, eecda943, 2026-08-31); CE's StreamRunning rides the same chain
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** For every result row, WireResultSink::EncodeProjectedRow and EncodeValueRow construct a fresh `std::vector<std::byte> bytes`, which is one malloc and free per row. wire::EncodeValue fills it one byte at a time through PutLE's push_back (row_codec.cpp:28-33). The row bytes are then copied as follows: 1. `out.assign` into the dispatcher's row_scratch string (kwp_session.cpp:246). 2. Emit into `open_` (kwp_session.cpp:278). 3. Seal into a newly allocated `batch.payload` (kwp_session.cpp:287-290). 4. wire::EncodeFrame into another new vector (frame_codec.cpp:49-52). 5. KwpSession::Send into `frames_out` (kwp_session.cpp:358). 6. FlushFrames then AppendReplyBytes into the `outbox` string (tcp_server.cpp:1068). 7. With TLS, once more into pending_plain. Each 64 KiB batch therefore costs two extra allocations and four full copies after it is sealed, and each row costs a malloc plus three copies before that. The dispatcher's walk calls EncodeProjectedRow and then Emit per row (command_dispatcher.cpp:12758-12762, and 7191, 9732, 9831, 11381, 11441).

**Evidence.** kwp_session.cpp:235-236 `std::vector<std::byte> bytes; bytes.reserve(projection.size() * 12);` kwp_session.cpp:246 `out.assign(reinterpret_cast<const char*>(bytes.data()), bytes.size());` frame_codec.cpp:49-52 `std::vector<std::byte> out; out.reserve(kFrameHeaderSize + payload.size()); ... out.insert(out.end(), payload.begin(), payload.end());`

**Suggested.** Mechanism of the gain: remove the per-row malloc and collapse the copies. Encode the row straight into the sink's open batch buffer, with the 8-byte frame header and the 2-byte row count reserved at its front and patched at Seal; EncodeValue already appends to a caller's vector, so a failed row is rolled back by resize to a mark as RowBatchWriter::AppendRow does. Then move the sealed buffer into the outbox, or append it once, instead of EncodeFrame, Send and AppendReplyBytes each copying it. Give PutLE a fixed-width append in place of byte-wise push_back. Measure in build-release as server CPU per row on a wide streaming SELECT (for example a 100k-row scan over KWP), interleaved A/B, and as allocations per row; a point SELECT's fixed cost should be watched as a control. No number is claimed.

### H44. Every read of sys.relation_stats and sys.indexes, SHOW BUDGET included, walks every leaf of every clustered tree or every entry of every index on the reactor

- **Kind**: performance; severity medium (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `sys-views` (`sys-views#2`)
- **Where**: `src/server/sys_views.cpp:830`; origin: pre-3.4 (BO) for the walks; DF-S3 (83aad532) and DF-S4 (29810582) put the pollable in-memory counters on the same two views
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** A view is materialized whole and then filtered (sys-views.md section 2), and these two builders compute their tree-shape columns unconditionally. RelationStatsView calls BtreeLeafCount, a next-leaf walk of the whole clustered tree, for every btree relation in the instance. IndexesView calls IndexEntryCount, a visit of every entry of every leaf, for every index. A projection or a WHERE does not prune it: SHOW BUDGET is defined as a SELECT of nine budget columns from sys.relation_stats, none of which needs a page beyond the sys.tables row, yet it pays the full leaf walk of every relation. DF placed the monitoring columns an agent polls on exactly these views (lock_waits, lock_wait_us, est_delete_marked, leaf_divisions, leaf_compactions, probes, ranges, last_used), all of which come from memory-resident stores. Reading them costs page reads proportional to the database size, through the one bounded pool, as blocking GetForRead calls on the session's reactor, with no yield, slice boundary or cancel point; HandleCatalogView is synchronous. The spec states the walk as a fact (section 6.2, "height and leaves walk the clustered tree") and DF's work order notes it; neither known-gaps.md nor the work order records the stall or that SHOW BUDGET and the DF counters ride on it.

**Evidence.** sys_views.cpp:830 `if (auto l = btree::BtreeLeafCount(page_store_, root.value()); l.ok()) {` inside the loop over ListTables(). sys_views.cpp:679 `auto entries = index::IndexEntryCount(page_store_, root, layout);` inside the loop over ListIndexes(). btree.cpp:1375-1391 is a `for (;;)` over next_page_id with store.GetForRead per leaf. sys-views.md section 5 defines SHOW BUDGET as `SELECT oid, namespace, name, next_id, ids_issued, ids_remaining, budget_used_ppm, budget_warning, budget_exhausted FROM sys.relation_stats`.

**Suggested.** Hand the builder the set of columns the statement projects or filters on, as the key conjunct is already handed, and skip the walk when leaves, height or entries are not referenced.
