Or split the walked columns into a keyed view (kChecked on the relation or index name) so the counter views stay memory-only. The gain is the removal of O(leaves) page reads and the reactor stall from every poll. Measure it in build-release as server CPU and the core's sched.run_queue wait for a concurrent point SELECT while a second session polls SHOW BUDGET and sys.indexes on a volume larger than buffer_pool_frames.

### H45. A hot relation's lock entry is one partition mutex taken three times per statement from every core; the third take, in WakeWaiters, is unconditional

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `arch-concurrency` (`arch-concurrency#3`)
- **Where**: `src/txn/lock_table.cpp:532`; origin: pre-3.4 (AO, BP-S6); CP-S2a (3.4.*) made the relation IS every read's one borrow
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** The table is partitioned by `(rel_oid, unit, lo)`, so one relation's own entry lives in exactly one of the 64 partitions. Every statement on that relation, on every core, takes that partition's `std::mutex` in `TryAcquire` (a writer's `IX`, or a read's `ReadBorrow` `IS`), again in `ReleaseHeld` to erase its holder, and a third time in `WakeWaiters`, which `ReleaseHeld` calls whenever a holder was removed, whether or not the entry has a waiter. In a single-table workload the 64 partitions therefore do not spread the relation-level traffic: it is one mutex and one `holders` vector, three lock/unlock pairs per statement per core. Each tuple unit also pays the unconditional second take at release. known-gaps.md:561-571 records the scan cost of `ReleaseHeld`/`WakeWaiters` at one core; neither it nor the pending multicore note names the re-acquisition or the relation-entry funnel.

**Evidence.** lock_table.cpp:502-532: the erase runs inside `{ Partition& part = PartitionFor(held.key); LatchGuard guard(part.latch); ... }`, then `if (removed_holder) WakeWaiters(held.key);`. WakeWaiters at :407 opens with its own `LatchGuard guard(part.latch); if (Entry* e = FindEntry(part, key)) { for (Tenant& w : e->waiters) FlipSlot(w, kick);`. lock_table.hpp header: the relation's own entry is "touched by every writer's `IX` and every positioned reader's `IS`". command_dispatcher.cpp:7558 builds a `ReadBorrow` over `locks_` for a read.

**Suggested.** Cheap step: in `ReleaseHeld`, record under the first hold whether the entry still has waiters and call `WakeWaiters` only then. A waiter registers under the latch that saw the refusing holder, so one that registers after the erase did not see this holder and is owed no flip from it. Structural step, if the funnel shows up in measurement: give the relation-level intention modes a per-core counted fast path in the shape of AO-R3's fence counter (raise the own-core count, then check an `X`-pending word, publish first and verify second), so `IS`/`IX` touch no shared mutex while no DDL is present. Measure with the `contention_*` kLockPartition wait tally and server CPU on one-table `point` and `insert1` cells at cores 4.

### H46. A resident point SELECT pays the sliced-walk scaffolding it is documented never to use: at least 7 coroutine frames, a make_shared<SelectRun> and a heap-allocating std::function per statement

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `arch-hot-path` (`arch-hot-path#1`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:10005`; origin: pre-3.4 (BA-S15, 0064d8bd, 2026-10-10; AwaitStatementWaits 8933e400, 2026-09-11)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** SelectBody states that a tuple key descent is never handed on because it 'cannot fill a slice' (10512-10527), yet the inline path still builds everything the hand-off exists for. Per point SELECT over KWP the path allocates: the DispatchAsync frame (940), the AwaitStatementWaits frame (646, called unconditionally at 1011 - its own body is a loop that finds nothing to wait for), std::make_shared<SelectRun> (10005), then in RunInline (7260) the SelectRun::Execute frame (7179), ExecuteChain (step_vm.cpp:3586), ChainRunner::Run (286), RunStep (620) and RunPointStep (666). RunPointStep contains no suspension at all (its body is `co_return AcceptByKey(...)`), while its sibling RunKeySetStep (685) is already a plain function 'Not a coroutine and never yields (CU-Q8)'. The row sink `visit` (7195-7196) captures `this`, `&scratch` and the `render` lambda (24 bytes), which is over libstdc++'s 16-byte std::function inline buffer, so constructing exec::RowSink allocates as well. include/kds/sched/coro.hpp defines no promise operator new, so every frame is a general-heap allocation. None of this is in known-gaps.md, the DE/DF cost notes or the F/G audits.

**Evidence.** command_dispatcher.cpp:10512 `**A tuple key descent is never handed on**: it reads one leaf and cannot fill a slice, so it would pay the hand-off - a frame, a hold, a pin - for a yield it never reaches.` step_vm.cpp:666-675 `sched::Coro RunPointStep(...) { auto key = KeyFromOperand(*step.key, frame_); ... co_return AcceptByKey(steps, index, step, access, key.value()); }` command_dispatcher.cpp:7195 `const exec::RowSink visit = [this, &scratch, render](const exec::ChainFrame& frame) -> ...`

**Suggested.** Three independent cuts, each measurable as server CPU per statement on the resident point-SELECT cell in build-release (interleaved A/B): (1) make RunPointStep a plain Status function called as `co_return RunPointStep(...)`, as RunKeySetStep is; (2) have DispatchAsync test `page_wait || write_block || lock_wait || select_run || build_run || parked_write` before calling AwaitStatementWaits so the common statement allocates no frame for it; (3) capture only `this` in `visit` (scratch is reachable through scratch_, render can be a member) so the std::function stays in its inline buffer. A larger step is a non-coroutine driver for a chain whose outer step is kLookup/kProbe with the SelectRun taken from the dispatcher's existing select_scratch_pool_ instead of make_shared.

### H47. An INSERT deep-copies every row's values (strings included) to drop the tuple key: once in InsertOneRow for a named key, and once more per row, for either arity, when the relation has a foreign key

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence high); round 1, `arch-hot-path` (`arch-hot-path#2`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:8309`; origin: pre-3.4 (InsertBodyOf cb99ef22, 2026-09-01; body_storage 61254092)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** InsertOneRow builds `body_storage` with `assign(values.begin() + 1, values.end())` for every row that names its tuple key - a vector allocation plus a copy of each parser::AstValue, which carries a std::string (ast.hpp:89), so every text value longer than the SSO buffer is a further allocation and memcpy. For a relation with outgoing foreign keys the extraction pass (7731-7736) calls InsertBodyOf per row first, which copies the row in both arities: the named-key arm builds a new vector and the key-omitted arm is `return values;`, a full copy of a vector the caller only reads. So a keyed row into a relation with a foreign key is deep-copied twice. The cost scales with rows and columns for a bulk VALUES and a KWP load chunk (which always carries the tuple key field), and is a fixed vector + N value copies on the single-row autocommit INSERT. Everything downstream only reads the body.

**Evidence.** command_dispatcher.cpp:8309-8310 `body_storage.assign(values.begin() + 1, values.end()); } const std::vector<parser::AstValue>& body = explicit_key ? body_storage : values;` command_dispatcher.cpp:5692 `if (ncols > 0 && values.size() == ncols - 1) return values;` command_dispatcher.cpp:7732 `const std::vector<parser::AstValue> body = InsertBodyOf(ta, values);`

**Suggested.** Pass the body as `std::span<const parser::AstValue>` (values.subspan(1) for a named key, values otherwise) to ResolveForeignKeyParents, the assertion admission, exec::EncodeRow, NoteCabinWrite, MaintainIndexes and ReserveInsert, and delete InsertBodyOf's copies. Measure on a multi-row INSERT with varchar columns and on the KWP load cell, with and without a foreign key.

### H48. WriteStatisticsAtShutdown re-walks the sys.statistics chain from its root for every row

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `catalog-delta` (`catalog-delta#1`)
- **Where**: `src/catalog/catalog.cpp:3974`; origin: CY-S1a (abe870fc); row count widened by CY-S1b's managed objects
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** The shutdown write inserts each statistics row with a separate InsertRow call, and InsertRow always starts at the chain root and fetches every full page before reaching the tail. Writing N rows over P pages therefore costs about N*P/2 page fetches instead of N. This is the only bulk writer of a catalog chain. ExportStatistics emits one prior row per relation plus, per managed relation, a slot row and up to ten version rows (published, pending, eight replaced).

**Evidence.** catalog.cpp:3970-3975 `for (const SysStatisticsRow& row : rows) { ... InsertRow(/*wal=*/nullptr, /*undo_hook=*/nullptr, store_, kCatalogPageStatistics, row, kBootstrapXid)`; InsertRow at catalog.cpp:480 `PageId current = root;`, then a `store.Get(current)` and a failed InsertTuple per full page before `current = next`. A row is 56 bytes of payload plus the 20-byte tuple header, so roughly 100 rows fit a page.

**Scenario.** No wrong result. On an instance with many relations under managed statistics (tens of thousands of rows, hundreds of chain pages), the shutdown tail performs millions of latched page fetches and failed slot probes before its sync. A few hundred relations cost nothing noticeable.

**Suggested.** Give the catalog a multi-row append that keeps the tail page across rows (carry `current` between inserts, or pass a start-page hint to InsertRow) and use it in WriteStatisticsAtShutdown. Measure as shutdown wall time on a volume with a large managed-statistics set, in build-release.

### H49. Every inserted row pays several transient heap allocations in the dispatcher: a deep row copy for the FK pass, a std::function for the spill log, and a fresh vector per WAL payload

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `dispatcher-2` (`dispatcher-2#2`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:7732`; origin: pre-3.4 (InsertBodyOf: AH-T1 cb99ef22; SpillLogFor: AT-S21 review d8893e23; LogInsert / LogIndexWrite buffers older)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** Four per-row costs on the INSERT path that buy nothing. (1) The FK extraction pass calls InsertBodyOf for every row of a relation with fkeys_out; both arms return a new std::vector<AstValue> by value (5689-5692), so each row is deep-copied (two std::string members per value) only so ResolveForeignKeyParents can index `body[fk.column_no - 1]`; InsertOneRow then copies a key-naming row again (`body_storage.assign`, ~8308) and InsertOnConflict copies every row a third way (8009). (2) SpillLogFor (6591-6607) returns a std::function whose capture is `[this, &scope, rel_oid, tuple_key, txn_id]` (40 bytes, over libstdc++'s 16-byte local storage), and InsertOneRow builds it for every row inside `encode` (8418), as the UPDATE path does (12262), whether or not the row has a spilling value. (3) LogInsert allocates and zero-fills `std::vector<std::byte> payload(kHeapWriteFixedSize + tuple.size())` per row (6718). (4) LogIndexWrite does the same per index entry (6579).

**Evidence.** 7732: `const std::vector<parser::AstValue> body = InsertBodyOf(ta, values);`  5692: `if (ncols > 0 && values.size() == ncols - 1) return values;`  6595: `return [this, &scope, rel_oid, tuple_key, txn_id](const exec::AppendedSpill& spill) -> Status {`  6718: `std::vector<std::byte> payload(wal::kHeapWriteFixedSize + tuple.size());`

**Suggested.** Pass ResolveForeignKeyParents a span over the row with the key offset (values.subspan(explicit_key ? 1 : 0)) instead of a copied vector, and let InsertOneRow use the same span rather than body_storage. Make the spill log a small struct or function_ref built once per statement (or only when the layout has a variable-width column), not a std::function per row. Encode BTREE_INSERT / INDEX_INSERT payloads into a dispatcher-owned scratch buffer that is resized, not reallocated and zero-filled, per record. Measure in build-release as server CPU per row on a multi-row INSERT and a KWP load into an FK child relation with a secondary index, interleaved A/B.

### H50. SelectRun::Execute's row sink is a std::function with a 24-byte capture, one heap allocation per SELECT

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `dispatcher-2` (`dispatcher-2#3`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:7195`; origin: pre-3.4 (BA-S15, 0064d8bd, 2026-10-10)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** `visit` is an exec::RowSink (std::function, step_vm.hpp:58) constructed from a lambda capturing `[this, &scratch, render]`, where `render` is itself a lambda capturing `this`. That is three pointers (24 bytes), above libstdc++'s 16-byte in-place limit, so every SELECT that reaches Execute - a resident point read included - allocates and frees the functor on the heap. `scratch` is `*scratch_` and `render` uses only members, so the sink needs nothing but `this`.

**Evidence.** 7185: `auto render = [this](const exec::ChainFrame& frame, std::string& into) {`  7195-7196: `const exec::RowSink visit = [this, &scratch, render](const exec::ChainFrame& frame) -> StatusOr<storage::VisitControl> {`  step_vm.hpp:58: `using RowSink = std::function<StatusOr<storage::VisitControl>(const ChainFrame&)>;`

**Suggested.** Capture only `this` (reach the scratch through `scratch_` and make `render` a private member function), which fits std::function's local storage and removes the allocation. Measure with the point-SELECT server-CPU A/B that DF and DE used; the expected gain is one malloc/free pair per statement, so it needs the sub-microsecond method recorded for the CX close bisect.

### H51. Every planned join SELECT copies the shared statistics snapshot's shared_ptr at its end (and at its compile), an atomic increment and decrement on one control block all cores share

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `dispatcher-3` (`dispatcher-3#2`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:10579`; origin: pre-3.4 (CC-S2, f3ced1ad, in v3.3.0); CX-Q2 extended the per-statement record to `fixed`, the 3.4 default
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** PlanStatsSource::Pin() returns `std::shared_ptr<const PlanStatsSnapshot>` by value (src/stats/plan_stats.cpp:739-747). Each core's cached_ is a copy of the one `current_` the instance publishes (plan_stats.cpp:593-596), so every Pin is a lock-prefixed increment, and its release a decrement, on a control block shared by every core. RecordPlanStats pins once per multi-relation, non-all-descent SELECT at its end under both `planned` and `fixed`; the compile pins again under `planned` or on a `fixed` miss (src/exec/step_compiler.cpp:2844-2845). Both uses are synchronous and on the owning core, where `cached_` already keeps the snapshot alive.

**Evidence.** command_dispatcher.cpp:10579: `const std::shared_ptr<const stats::PlanStatsSnapshot> snapshot = plan_source_.Pin();` plan_stats.cpp:746: `return cached_;` (a copy). The header's own comment says 'one acquire load a pin' (plan_stats.hpp:271-273), which counts the generation load and not the reference count write.

**Suggested.** Have Pin() return `const PlanStatsSnapshot*` (or a const reference to cached_): the source is one core's, cached_ is replaced only by that core's next Pin, and neither RecordPlanStats nor the compile suspends while reading it. The gain is the removal of a cross-core cache-line write per join statement; it would be measured as server CPU per statement on a two-relation walked join at cores >= 4 in build-release, interleaved A/B.

### H52. RecordAccessShapes reads the clock at every successful SELECT's end although the statement's head instant is already in hand

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `dispatcher-3` (`dispatcher-3#3`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:10627`; origin: pre-3.4 call (present at v3.4.0:8100), made redundant by DF-S1's statement_head_ns_ and kept by DA-S1 (89c26252)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** With access statistics on, every successful SELECT (plain, aggregated, ANALYZE, nested) calls NowNs() - a virtual Clock::Now() into the monotonic clock - only to stamp `last_seen`. DF added `statement_head_ns_`, read once at the statement head (command_dispatcher.cpp:1270), and RecordIndexUse, called three lines later from the same RecordExecution, stamps `last_used` from it (10568); DF's close removed exactly this kind of read from the index fold and known-gaps.md:2169-2171 records the accepted semantics ('last_used is a statement's head, not the run's own instant'). The access-shape stamp was not given the same treatment.

**Evidence.** command_dispatcher.cpp:10627: `stats::RecordChainAccess(*access_stats_, core_id_, chain, static_cast<std::uint64_t>(NowNs()));` against 10568: `static_cast<std::uint64_t>(statement_head_ns_));`

**Suggested.** Pass statement_head_ns_ as the stamp, with the same stated caveat as `last_used` (a walk resumed after a park stamps the head its core staged last). One clock read less per SELECT; measured as server CPU on a resident point SELECT in build-release, where DF-S1's bar was set.

### H53. UPDATE re-fetches the leaf it already holds for every row, on a stated premise the pool no longer has, and allocates two buffers per updated row

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `dispatcher-4` (`dispatcher-4#4`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:12329`; origin: pre-3.4 (the re-fetch and its comment predate BE's fixed frame slots; the WAL buffer is from the HEAP_OVERWRITE logging work)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** For every row an UPDATE writes, `apply` (a) calls page_store_.Get(page_id) and builds a second PageView on the leaf the walk or the descent already holds for write and passed in as `page`; the comment gives the reason as 'a store is free to move its frames when it hands out a new page', which is no longer true of the pool - frames are slots in chunks that never move (eviction.md, CLAUDE.md's buffer-pool row) - and WriteWalk::Claim's own argument is that the walk holds a live write PageRef for the page throughout; (b) heap-allocates the new image (`exec::EncodeAssigned` returns a fresh std::vector, :12269) and (c) heap-allocates and zero-fills a second vector for the HEAP_OVERWRITE payload (:12385) into which the image is copied before wal_->Append copies it again. The function keeps `values`, `assigned`, `row`, `spills` and `payload_copy` statement-lived precisely so that 'after the first row it does not allocate' (:11932-11934), and these two are the buffers that still do.

**Evidence.** command_dispatcher.cpp:12288-12291 `// Re-fetched rather than written through `page`: encoding may have appended to the var-heap, and a store is free to move its frames when it hands out a new page`; :12329 `auto page_again = page_store_.Get(page_id);`; :12385 `std::vector<std::byte> buf(wal::kHeapWriteFixedSize + encoded.value().size());`.

**Suggested.** Write through `page` (or keep the Get only behind a debug assertion that it returns the same frame) and correct the comment; make the WAL payload buffer a statement-lived scratch resized once (the row size is a schema constant, invariant 13), and give EncodeAssigned an out-parameter overload that fills a statement-lived image buffer. Measure with BN's every-row UPDATE cell in build-release, interleaved A/B, server CPU per row. The DELETE half of this (its re-read and copy) is F24 and is not repeated here; F24's open question - whether the leaf hold is continuous from qualify to write - is the same question for (a) and is what WriteWalk::Claim's comment asserts.

### H54. The controller's seeded build decodes every column and fetches every spilled value of every row to read one column

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `exec-cabin-transition` (`exec-cabin-transition#4`)
- **Where**: `src/exec/cabin_optimizer_exec.cpp:260`; origin: pre-3.4 (not blamed)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** BuildSeededSets copies each live row's whole payload out of the leaf, runs the full DecodeRow, then ResolveSpills for every spilled cell, and uses only decoded[col_pos]. On a relation with spilled varchar columns the background build reads a var-heap page per spilled value, through the ordinary pool rather than the scan ring, which works against PO4's stated aim that the build not displace the foreground working set. row_codec.hpp already offers DecodeColumnsInto for a column mask.

**Evidence.** cabin_optimizer_exec.cpp:260 `auto decoded = DecodeRow(access.schema, access.layout, row.payload, &spills);`, 262 `ResolveSpills(store_, spills, decoded.value())`, 266 `MakeCabinKey(cabin_id, decoded.value()[col_pos])`. row_codec.hpp:320 `DecodeColumnsInto decodes only the columns `columns` selects`.

**Suggested.** Decode only col_pos with DecodeColumnsInto and resolve a spill only when that cell is spilled. The gain is fewer var-heap page fetches and allocations per row during CREATE and EXTEND; measure pages fetched and tick duration for a build over a relation with spilled columns, with cabin_optimizer on.

### H55. ORDER BY <driving tuple key>, <more keys> keeps a full sort (and its sort_max_rows refusal) though the trailing keys cannot change the order

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `exec-compiler` (`exec-compiler#1`)
- **Where**: `src/exec/step_compiler.cpp:3613`; origin: pre-3.4 for the ascending elision; CV-S1 (dd1d3f4a) and CV-S2 (10e7b0a4) repeated the one-key / last-key shape for the reverse walk and DriverOrderOf
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** Both elisions demand that the tuple key be the only sort key (section 5a) or the last one (DriverOrderOf). A tuple key is unique per relation (hard invariant 11), so once the driving relation's tuple key is a sort key every later key is irrelevant when it is a column of that same relation: rows sharing the driving tuple key are one driver row's join fan-out and carry identical slot-0 values. `SELECT ... FROM t ORDER BY id, created_at` therefore materialises and sorts (and without a LIMIT is refused past sort_max_rows) where `ORDER BY id` streams from the walk; `ORDER BY id DESC, x LIMIT n` misses the reverse walk; and `ORDER BY a, id, b LIMIT n` misses the ordered index walk because line 1368 returns nullopt for a tuple key that is not last.

**As narrowed by verification.** A slot-0 sort key written after the driving relation's tuple key cannot change the order, yet it defeats all three elisions (step_compiler.cpp:3613 size()==1; DriverOrderOf 1367-1369), so such a statement sorts and, without a LIMIT, can be refused by sort_max_rows where `ORDER BY id` streams. This matches parser-v2.md I11 as written ('alone'; 'every other order costing an output sort'), so it is an unbuilt optimisation rather than a defect. Any fix in DriverOrderOf must still check that the keys after the tuple key are slot-0 columns, because a key of another slot orders the fan-out and must keep the sort.

**Evidence.** 3613: `const bool one_tuple_key = chain.sort_keys.size() == 1 && chain.sort_keys[0].ref.rel_slot == 0 && IsTupleKey(chain.sort_keys[0].ref) && !chain.steps.empty();` 1367-1369: `if (IsTupleKey(ref.value())) { if (k + 1 != stmt.order_by.size()) return std::nullopt; break; }` No entry for this in known-gaps.md or the CV work order (grepped 'trailing key', 'redundant.*sort', 'ORDER BY.*tuple key.*,').

**Scenario.** Not a wrong answer. Cost and a refusal: over a relation larger than sort_max_rows, `SELECT * FROM t ORDER BY id, name` is refused by the sort cap while the equivalent `ORDER BY id` answers by streaming.

**Suggested.** In section 5a, when sort_keys[0] is the slot-0 tuple key, drop every following key whose ref is a slot-0 column before testing the elisions (keys naming another slot must stay: they order a driver row's fan-out). In DriverOrderOf, treat a tuple key at position k as ending the order - every key DriverOrderOf accepts is already a slot-0 column - instead of returning nullopt when it is not last. Measure in build-release with an `ORDER BY id, <col>` cell beside `ORDER BY id`, with and without LIMIT, and add a byte-for-byte cell against the sorted answer.

### H56. Forward ordered index walk pays a full descent and a redundant leaf read for every leaf that held a match

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `exec-vm` (`exec-vm#4`)
- **Where**: `src/exec/step_vm.cpp:1838`; origin: CV-S2 (10e7b0a4), rule CV-R3 / index.md IX8c
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** After each batch, the walk re-seeks from the last entry of the leaf it just read. IndexSeekLeaf descends root to leaf, lands on that same leaf and returns only its id; IndexFetchLeaf then fetches it again, every entry is skipped as at or below `from`, and only then is the right sibling taken. Per result leaf that is depth + 1 extra page fetches and latch acquisitions for one useful leaf read. The function's own argument for following `right_sibling` within a pass (1812-1815: an entry above the leaf's last was already to its right when the leaf was read, and entries only move right) holds equally after ResolveBatch, so the re-seek protects nothing that the pointer read under the hold does not.

**As narrowed by verification.** The cost is real and as counted. After every pass that produced a batch, RunOrderedIndexStep re-descends root to leaf for `from`, fetches that same leaf a second time, skips every entry, and only then reads the right sibling. That is tree height + 1 page fetches beyond the one useful leaf read, per result leaf. The redundant re-read also counts toward `pages_fetched` and `slice_pages_`, so an outermost ordered walk reaches its slice boundary after about half as many useful leaves. The re-seek is not an oversight: it is the documented rule CV-R3 and index.md IX8c ("sought again from the last entry the leaf held, never reached by pointer"), and CV-S0 priced a re-descent per leaf as its premise. What is new is narrower: the forward walk's per-leaf descent is not recorded in known-gaps.md (only the reverse walks' is, lines 2868-2876 and 2894-2897), is not priced, and was not measured across many leaves. The assertion that the re-seek "protects nothing" is not established: the within-pass sibling hop never spans the resolve interval or a slice yield, and the pointer-carried step would. Removing it needs the operator's ruling on CV-R3.

**Evidence.** 1838: `auto first = index::IndexSeekLeaf(store_, ix->root_page_id, layout, from);` 1847: `auto held = index::IndexFetchLeaf(store_, cur, layout, storage::PageAccess::kRead);` 1813-1815: `every entry the view needs was in the tree before the walk began, and one above the leaf's last was already to its right when the leaf was read`

**Scenario.** No wrong result. known-gaps.md records the reverse walks' descent per leaf but not this forward cost, and CV's close measured LIMIT 20 windows served from one to a few leaves, where it does not show.

**Suggested.** Carry `right` (read under the leaf's hold) out of the pass and start the next pass there, keeping the `LowerBound(from)` skip as the guard, so the seek is paid only for the first pass. This changes CV-R3 and IX8c as written, so it needs the operator's ruling. The gain would be measured in build-release on an ordered walk crossing many index leaves (a deep OFFSET, or a residual that rejects most resolved rows), as server CPU and page fetches per statement; it will be small where the per-entry BtreeLookup dominates.

### H57. Every transaction head reads each core's load slot, on the cache line that core writes every statement and every reactor iteration

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `expeditor-session` (`expeditor-session#1`)
- **Where**: `include/kds/sched/core_load.hpp:50`; origin: pre-3.4 (BL-S1 / BL-S3, v3.0.0)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** Above one core, LoadPlacer::Place runs at every transaction head (every autocommit statement and every BEGIN) and loads busy_ppm, active_sessions and window_end_ns from every core's CoreLoad::Slot. Those three words sit in the slot's first 64 bytes together with words the owning core stores far more often: queued_foreground (every reactor iteration), heads / inside (every statement) and active_now. So each head on any core takes up to cores-1 coherence misses, and each owner's next store to its own slot must take the line back exclusive. The cost grows with cores squared across the instance. The slot is also not the 'one cache-line slot' its header and rules.md line 46 call it: six u32 plus six u64 is 72 bytes, padded to 128 by alignas(64).

**Evidence.** core_load.hpp:50-83: `struct alignas(64) Slot { busy_ppm; foreground_ppm; queued_foreground; sessions; active_sessions; active_now; heads; inside; window_end_ns; moved_out_in; moves_out; partition_moves_out; }` - offsets 0 to 63 hold everything but partition_moves_out. load_placer.cpp:50-54: `for (k < n) { view[k] = {s.busy_ppm.load, s.active_sessions.load, s.window_end_ns.load}; }`, called from tcp_server.cpp:685-687 on every head. Writers on the same line: scheduler.cpp:516 `load.queued_foreground.store(...)` each iteration; tcp_server.cpp:673 `CoreLoad::Bump(head ? load.heads : load.inside)` each statement.

**Suggested.** Split the slot into a published line (busy_ppm, active_sessions, window_end_ns, written once per 50 ms window) and an owner line (queued_foreground, heads, inside, active_now, sessions, the move counters). Separately, let Place return before the snapshot when the partition is inactive and the cheap exits already hold: its own moved_out_in equals its window, or the session is inside its dwell. Measure in build-release as server CPU per statement on a point-read workload at 4 and 8 cores, interleaved A/B.

### H58. Every index seek fetches its landing leaf twice: the descent's held leaf is dropped and re-fetched by id

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `index-fk-assert-delta` (`index-fk-assert-delta#2`)
- **Where**: `src/storage/index/index_tree.cpp:570`; origin: pre-3.4 for the probe and unique-check sites; CV-S2 10e7b0a4 added the ordered run's site and CV-S1 dd1d3f4a the clustered reverse step's, while CV-S3 7d11ba44 built the held-leaf form only for its own step
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**
- **Recorded in**: instructions/v3.2.0/workorder-cv-ordered-index-walk.md, CV-S1 review 'Not applied' (lines 378-381) - the clustered reverse step's second fetch only; the forward index sites are not recorded anywhere I found

**Claim (the finder's).** `IndexSeekLeaf` runs `DescendTo(..., leaf_for_write=false)`, which returns the leaf pinned and read-latched in `Descent::leaf`, then returns only its page id, releasing the leaf. Every caller immediately fetches that id again: `IndexVisitFrom` -> `IndexFetchLeaf` for an index probe (step_vm.cpp:1546-1552) and for the unique-key check (index_maintain.cpp:330-333), and `IndexFetchLeaf` in the ordered forward run (step_vm.cpp:1838, 1847), once per pass. That is one extra buffer-pool lookup, pin/unpin and shared page-latch acquire/release, plus a repeat of the type and width checks, on every index probe. CV-S3 already wrote the alternative for the reverse run (`IndexFetchLeafAtOrBelow`: "The descent's own leaf, held: no second fetch of it"), so the held-leaf return exists and only the forward paths still use the id-returning form. The clustered twin `BtreeFetchLeafAtOrBelow` (btree.cpp:1311-1317) repeats the pattern per reverse step: `BtreeSeekLeaf` then `BtreeFetchLeaf(current, access)` of the same leaf (there the second fetch also changes the access class from the descent's read to the walk's kScan, which a held-leaf form would have to carry). `IndexFetchLeafAtOrBelow` itself fetches the right sibling twice per hop (once in `LeafStillCoversKey`, once in `IndexFetchLeaf`).

**As narrowed by verification.** Each forward index seek fetches its landing leaf twice: IndexSeekLeaf (index_tree.cpp:570-582) discards the read-held leaf DescendTo returned and gives back only its id, and its three callers re-fetch that id at once under the same kRead access - the index probe (step_vm.cpp:1546-1552 via IndexVisitFrom), the unique-key check (index_maintain.cpp:330-333) and the CV-S2 forward ordered run (step_vm.cpp:1838/1847, once per pass). The reverse ordered run already uses the held leaf (IndexFetchLeafAtOrBelow, CV-S3). The same pattern in the clustered reverse step (BtreeFetchLeafAtOrBelow, btree.cpp:1309-1327) is already recorded as a not-applied review item left to measurement in workorder-cv-ordered-index-walk.md CV-S1 (lines 378-381) and was never measured in isolation. The double fetch of the right sibling in IndexFetchLeafAtOrBelow happens only on a move-right hop, which is rare. Cost per seek: one resident-page pool lookup, pin and shared-latch round trip and a repeated type/width check; unmeasured and likely near this host's noise floor.

**Evidence.** index_tree.cpp:579-581: `auto descent = DescendTo(store, root, layout, sort_key, /*leaf_for_write=*/false); ... return descent.value().path[descent.value().depth];`. step_vm.cpp:1838 and 1847: `auto first = index::IndexSeekLeaf(store_, ix->root_page_id, layout, from);` ... `auto held = index::IndexFetchLeaf(store_, cur, layout, storage::PageAccess::kRead);`. index_tree.cpp:604-606: `// The descent's own leaf, held: no second fetch of it.` / `storage::PageRef leaf = std::move(descent.value().leaf);`.

**Suggested.** Give the read seek a held-leaf form (return `Descent::leaf` with its id, as `IndexFetchLeafAtOrBelow` does) and let `IndexVisitFrom` / the ordered run start from that held leaf, re-checking nothing; the correctness argument is unchanged because a held leaf cannot divide. Measure as server CPU per statement on an index point probe and on a unique-index INSERT, build-release, interleaved A/B; the gain is one pool lookup and one latch round trip per seek, so it may sit near the noise floor of this host and should be measured before it is built.

### H59. Inbound path: front-erase of the frame buffer per popped frame is quadratic under a deep pipeline, and both listeners read 4 KiB per readiness event

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `net-wire` (`net-wire#4`)
- **Where**: `src/wire/frame_codec.cpp:112`; origin: pre-3.4 (f53b26ec, 2026-07-29; the 4 KiB reads from the original TcpServer and KwpLoadServer)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** Three front-erase and small-read sites on the wire path: 1. FrameDecoder::PopFrame copies the payload out (`frame.payload.assign`) and then erases the consumed prefix of `buffer_`, which moves the whole remainder forward on every frame. TcpServer keeps reading and feeding the decoder while a statement is in flight (OnClientReadable calls DrainFrames, which feeds the decoder but pops nothing while `conn.in_flight`), so a client that pipelines many small frames builds a large buffer. Draining B buffered bytes of f-byte frames then costs O(B^2 / f) bytes moved. 2. TcpServer::OnClientReadable (tcp_server.cpp:502-503) and KwpLoadServer::OnClientReadable (kwp_load_server.cpp:251-252) issue exactly one read() of 4096 bytes per readable event. A 256 KiB load chunk (kKwpMaxChunkBytes) or a large INSERT text therefore takes 64 or more reactor iterations, each with its own epoll round, and each read is copied inbox to decoder to payload. 3. TcpServer::FlushOutbox ends with `conn.outbox.erase(0, sent)` (tcp_server.cpp:832), which moves the unsent tail on every partial send. That is linear per writable event in the size of a large reply that was not streamed (a capped or query-layer result delivered whole).

**Evidence.** frame_codec.cpp:110-112 `frame.payload.assign(buffer_.begin() + kFrameHeaderSize, buffer_.begin() + total_size);` then `buffer_.erase(buffer_.begin(), buffer_.begin() + static_cast<std::ptrdiff_t>(total_size));` tcp_server.cpp:502-503 `char chunk[4096]; ssize_t n = ::read(client_fd, chunk, sizeof(chunk));` tcp_server.cpp:832 `conn.outbox.erase(0, sent);`

**Suggested.** Mechanism of the gain: keep a read cursor in FrameDecoder and in the outbox, compacting only when the consumed prefix exceeds half the buffer, so a pop or a partial send no longer moves the remainder. Read with a larger buffer (64 KiB), or loop read() a bounded number of times per event, so a load chunk or a large statement arrives in a few reactor turns. Measure in build-release: KWP load throughput (rows/s and server CPU over kwp_port at 256 KiB chunks), a pipelined point-read driver with a deep window (reactor CPU in memmove), and time to first and last byte of a large reply that is not streamed. No number is claimed.

### H60. A reader lease's register, refresh and release republish all three core bounds: two scans of live_ and an atomic exchange for values a lease cannot change

- **Kind**: performance; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence high); round 1, `txn-locks-delta` (`txn-locks-delta#2`)
- **Where**: `src/txn/manager.cpp:637`; origin: pre-3.4 (AN/BP); CS (adbea55b) added RefreshReader as a third lease site and DF-S2 (c2925b6e) widened the scan with the stamp
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** RegisterReader (manager.cpp:779), RefreshReader (:791) and UnregisterReader (:796) each call PublishCoreBounds, which recomputes and stores the snapshot bound (LocalSnapshotBound: a walk of every unique_ptr<Transaction> in live_ plus the lease bitmap) and then also recomputes and republishes the unresolved bound and the issue cursor (OldestActiveTrxId: a second walk of live_; PublishOldestUnresolved: an atomic exchange plus a NoteSlot; PublishIssueCursor). A lease holds no id and is not in live_, so the second walk and the exchange can never publish a changed value on these three paths. Every autocommit SELECT takes the pair (register at its head, release at its end), and every READ COMMITTED statement of a READ ONLY transaction takes the refresh; each costs two pointer-chasing passes over the core's open read-write transactions (up to 1,024 a core) and one lock-prefixed exchange for nothing. The DF work order (section 6 of its premise) records that the bound is recomputed whole and written up to three times a point SELECT, but known-gaps carries no entry and nothing states that two of the three publications are constant on the lease paths.

**Evidence.** manager.cpp:642-661 `const HeldBound bound = LocalSnapshotBound(); visibility_->PublishSnapshotBound(core_, bound.lsn, bound.minted_ns); ... visibility_->PublishBounds(core_, OldestActiveTrxId(), ids_.peek());`; :794-797 `void TransactionManager::UnregisterReader(...) { reader_used_[slot / 64] &= ...; PublishCoreBounds(); }`; instance_visibility.cpp:45 `const std::uint64_t was = slots_[core].oldest_unresolved.exchange(trx_id, ...)`.

**Suggested.** Give the lease paths a snapshot-only publication (LocalSnapshotBound + PublishSnapshotBound), leaving PublishCoreBounds to Begin, the decide and the burn. A further step is to keep the live transactions' minimum incrementally (ids are issued ascending per core, so the oldest active id is the first active entry) so that no publication walks live_. Measure as server CPU per point autocommit SELECT at cores = 1 with 0, 64 and 1,000 idle open explicit transactions on the core.

## 6.5 Architecture (16)

### H61. About 57 dispatcher refusals are built as bare "ERR ..." strings with no Status, so every one reaches a KWP/1 client (and sys.refusal_stats) as InvalidArgument - role denials, the aborted-transaction state and RESTRICT refusals included

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `arch-config-errors` (`arch-config-errors#1`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:1679`; origin: pre-3.4 pattern; 3.4 added sites to it (CX: the plan RESTRICT refusals at 4207, 4324, 4930; CS adbea55bd: 12856 'permission: BEGIN READ WRITE')
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** protocol.md section 11 says the category is 'the engine's own, carried and not re-parsed' and that StatusFromErrorReply's fold into InvalidArgument 'is only the floor for an outcome that carried no failing Status'. A grep of the dispatcher finds 57 `return {"ERR ...", false}` sites that carry no Status at all, so the floor is the live path for whole refusal classes a client would branch on: the role check (1679, 10096, 12856, 13199), 'current transaction is aborted; commands are ignored until ROLLBACK' (1723, 12926), 'a transaction is already open' (12779), 'no transaction is open' (12920, 13027), 'cannot change the isolation level inside a transaction' (13142, 13172), and the RESTRICT refusals of DROP INDEX / DROP CABIN / DROP TABLE / ALTER against a plan or an assertion (4207, 4324, 4467, 4881, 4892, 4930). All of them reach S_ERROR as category InvalidArgument ('simply wrong'), though none is fixed by fixing the statement's arguments - the rule section 11 itself gives for earning a category. RefusalCodeOf (line 322) does the same fold for sys.refusal_stats, so a permission denial and an ignored statement in an aborted transaction are counted as InvalidArgument there. Known-gaps records only the missing byte on six catalog-view refusals (line 2046), not the lost code.

**As narrowed by verification.** About two dozen of the dispatcher's 57 Status-less refusals are not argument errors - four role denials, two aborted-transaction answers, five transaction-state refusals, six or seven RESTRICT refusals, and six catalog-view 'not supported'/'no such view' refusals - and each reaches a KWP/1 client and sys.refusal_stats as InvalidArgument retryable=0 through StatusFromErrorReply's bare arm. This is the documented floor (command_dispatcher.hpp:162-167), previously read and dismissed by the F audit, and no existing StatusCode fits a role denial or a transaction-state refusal, so nothing is miscoded against a code that exists except the catalog-view refusals (which have Unsupported/NotImplemented/NotFound available). The header's 'handful' and protocol.md section 11's 'only the floor' understate how many client-distinguishable classes take the floor; adding categories is an operator decision.

**Evidence.** 1679: `return {"ERR permission: " + std::string(cmd) + " needs " + ...` / 1723: `return {"ERR current transaction is aborted; commands are ignored until ROLLBACK", false};` / 340-342: `// The bare arm. kInvalidArgument stands for every code that renders bare` ... `return Status::InvalidArgument(std::string(reply));` / 346: `return !outcome.status.ok() ? outcome.status : StatusFromErrorReply(outcome.response);`

**Scenario.** A KWP/1 client library on a readonly connection sends UPDATE, or sends any statement after a failed statement inside BEGIN: both answers are S_ERROR category InvalidArgument retryable=0, indistinguishable by code from a typo; the library can only tell 'ROLLBACK and retry the transaction' or 'wrong role' from 'rewrite the statement' by matching message text.

**Suggested.** Route every refusal through Refused(Status) so no site carries a line without a Status (the comment at 351-353 already states that intent), and decide per class whether it is InvalidArgument or earns a code: at minimum the aborted-transaction state and the role denial. The decision is the operator's (it appends wire categories); until then the spec's 'only the floor' sentence overstates.

### H62. Per-statement state lives in ~20 dispatcher members under a 'one statement at a time on a core' premise the same header says no longer holds

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence high); round 1, `arch-dispatcher-shape` (`arch-dispatcher-shape#1`)
- **Where**: `include/kds/server/command_dispatcher.hpp:3004`; origin: pre-3.4 (the premise retired at BA-S15); 3.4.* added four more members on the same terms: statement_plan_source_/label_ (CX-S1b 4f12b44d), statement_head_ns_/compile_ns_ (DF-S1 44dddd27), statement_binding_ (DI-S1 1ffbf607)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** The running statement's facts (pending_commit_lsn_, blocking_writer_, blocked_tuple_key_, lock_wait_, blocking_wake_, last_refusal_ + detail, statement_trail_mark_, statement_fetch_id_, statement_pattern_id_, statement_binding_, statement_plan_source_, statement_plan_label_, statement_head_ns_, statement_compile_ns_, effective_durability_, may_park_, reruns_structural_, structural_poison_withheld_, parking_statement_, statement_boundary_taken_) are class members, justified three times by 'one statement runs at a time on a core' (hpp:2678, 2817, 3000), while hpp:2708-2710 states another statement of the core runs inside a SELECT's walk or any park. Correctness therefore rests on every suspension site hand-saving and hand-restoring the subset it needs, with nothing enforcing it.

**As narrowed by verification.** The per-statement dispatcher members are sound inside a statement's head: StageStatement's body is synchronous and no co_await sits inside a MayParkScope window, so 'one statement at a time' holds for the span in which they are live. The exposure is only code that runs outside a head frame (resumed SelectRun/BuildRun slices, the KWP load path) reading those members, plus the hand-carried state a parked write restores on re-dispatch (trail mark, binding, boundary). One such read is a recorded gap (statement_head_ns_ in RecordIndexUse, known-gaps.md 'last_used'); nothing enforces that a resumed slice reads no frame member. A structural/maintainability point, not a defect at HEAD.

**Evidence.** hpp:3004 'Per-statement members, on `pending_commit_lsn_`'s terms (one statement at a time on a core), zeroed at the top of `DispatchAndStage`' against hpp:2708 'another statement of this core can run inside a `SELECT`'s walk, or inside any park'. StageStatement zeroes 14 of them (cpp:1258-1271) and exchanges them out (cpp:1294-1355). The hand-kept restores: Session::ParkedWrite carries trail_mark and binding back in (cpp:10868, 10873, 10923-10926), RefuseParkedWrite restores the mark (cpp:13287), BuildRun's tail re-enters StageStatement and StampStatement (cpp:3184-3189), ExecuteInsert stamps statement_head_ns_ itself (cpp:7394), MayParkScope/RerunScope save two more. One leak of this class is already recorded (known-gaps.md:2169, RecordIndexUse reading statement_head_ns_ at cpp:10568 after a park). The shared `aggregator_` (hpp:2879) is safe only because RunAggregated never yields (cpp:9810), whereas the sorter/trail/replay/stats beside it had to be pooled per run (SelectScratch, hpp:2917).

**Scenario.** No wrong result shown at HEAD beyond the recorded last_used one. The hazard is the next member: a field read by a resumed SelectRun/BuildRun slice, or a walk that starts yielding (an aggregated SELECT sliced like a plain one would have its fold reset by another session's aggregate on the same core), is silently another statement's value.

**Suggested.** Cut a `StatementFrame` struct holding exactly the members StageStatement zeroes plus effective_durability_/may_park_/reruns_structural_/parking_statement_/statement_boundary_taken_; StageStatement owns one on its stack and installs it through a scope (`frame_` pointer swapped and restored, as MayParkScope already does for two fields); SelectRun, BuildRun and Session::ParkedWrite hold theirs across the suspension instead of copying fields in and out. Handlers keep reading `frame_->x`, so no signature changes; the suspend audit becomes 'does this run own its frame' instead of a per-field list.

### H63. Three responsibility groups with a narrow member footprint sit inside the class: sys.meta rendering (seven members exist only for it), plan statements, statistics statements

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence high); round 1, `arch-dispatcher-shape` (`arch-dispatcher-shape#3`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:2140`; origin: MetaSections pre-3.4 (BO-S2 a8d1425e), grown by 3.4.* (DF, CX, DE sections); plan statements CX (3.4.*); statistics statements CY (3.4.*)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** A script mapping each large function to the data members it touches shows three cuts that share almost nothing with statement execution. (a) MetaSections (614 lines) reads 29 members, and superblock_, recovery_ (27 uses), scheduler_view_ (18), reclaim_counters_, cabin_executor_ are read by no other function in either dispatcher TU (cabin_controller_ and cabin_optimizer_view_latch_ only by MetaSections and CabinOptimizerView): the dispatcher holds the instance's recovery report, reactor, superblock and reclaim counters solely to print them. It renders 'key=value' text that MetaView immediately re-parses by splitting on ' ' and '='. (b) HandleSetJoinOrder, HandleDropPlan, HandleCreatePlan, HandleAlterPlan, HandleAdvisePlan, PlanDdlBusy, HoldPlanRelations, PlanExampleText, OrderText (about 650 lines) touch only admin_plans_, captured_plans_, plan_advices_, plan_stats_/plan_source_, budget_, sort_max_rows_, catalog_, locks_, wal_/page_store_ and four helpers (AwaitDdlDurability, ResolveNames, NextReadHolder, StatementContextFor). (c) HandleStatistics (267 lines) touches only log_ and plan_stats_, with ParseStatisticsDocument and GatherRun beside it.

**Evidence.** cpp:2140 `CommandDispatcher::MetaSections()`; sys_views.cpp:1884-1900 `for (const auto& [section, text] : MetaSections()) { ... const std::size_t eq = token.find('='); ... return Status::Corruption("sys.meta field '" ...` (a writer that emits a value containing a space turns sys.meta into a Corruption refusal). Member map (script): `recovery_ | MetaSections:27`, `scheduler_view_ | MetaSections:18`, `superblock_ | MetaSections:4`, `HandleStatistics MEMBERS: log_ plan_stats_`, `HandleAlterPlan MEMBERS: admin_plans_ catalog_ log_ page_store_ wal_`. Plan statements at cpp:2779, 2826, 14479-14988; statistics at cpp:3565-4020.

**Suggested.** (a) A `MetaSource` (the seven view-only pointers plus const accessors for the dispatcher's own per-core counters) with `AppendRows(CatalogView&)` emitting {section, field, value} rows directly in sys_views.cpp; the seven members, their setters and the text round trip leave the class. (b) `server/plan_statements.cpp`: free functions over a small `PlanDdlEnv {AdminPlans*, CapturedPlans*, PlanAdvices*, PlanStatsSource, Catalog&, LockTable*}` plus a callback for the durability park. (c) `server/statistics_statements.cpp` over `PlanStats*` and a relation resolver. Each cut removes a block from the 15,675-line TU that 89 test files and every statement path recompile against, without touching a walk.

### H64. The statement's leading verb is classified by string-compare lists at six sites kept in step by comments, and the statistics list has already drifted: ADVISE, GATHER, LOCK, UNLOCK, PUBLISH, RESTORE, EXPORT, IMPORT and DISCARD are routed but recorded as OTHER

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence high); round 1, `arch-hot-path` (`arch-hot-path#3`)
- **Where**: `src/stats/statement_stats.cpp:14`; origin: list from BO-S5 (pre-3.4); the drift from CX-S7 (ADVISE) and CY-S1b/CY-S4 (the statistics verbs), both 3.4.*
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** One fact - which verb this statement is - is recomputed by IEquals chains in RequiredRole (command_dispatcher.cpp:1600), the archive gate (1691), Session::AdmittedWhileFailed (session.hpp:456), ReadOnlyAdmits (189), the routing chain itself (1748-1984) and, at the statement's end, stats::StatementKindOf (statement_stats.cpp:32), which re-tokenizes the line and scans kKinds. Each list's contract with the routing is a comment ('Keyed on exactly the tokens DispatchInner routes on'; 'Every first word `DispatchInner` routes'). kKinds no longer holds that: DispatchInner routes ADVISE (1849) and GATHER/LOCK/UNLOCK/PUBLISH/RESTORE/EXPORT/IMPORT/DISCARD (1946-1951), none of which is in kKinds, so sys.statement_stats gives them kind `OTHER` although docs/spec/sys-views.md:194 says kind 'is a verb the dispatcher routes, else `OTHER`'. All of them therefore also share one fetch_id-0 row. The cost side is small but per statement: a SELECT passes about 30 case-insensitive compares before it routes (role 2, archive 9, then BEGIN...DROP, INSERT, SELECT) and up to 28 more in StatementKindOf under no latch.

**Evidence.** statement_stats.cpp:12-19 `// Every first word `DispatchInner` routes, in the spelling a row carries.` ... kKinds[] = {"SELECT", "WITH", ... "REINDEX", "WAYSTONE"} (no ADVISE, GATHER, LOCK, UNLOCK, PUBLISH, RESTORE, EXPORT, IMPORT, DISCARD) command_dispatcher.cpp:1946-1950 `(IEquals(cmd, "GATHER") || IEquals(cmd, "LOCK") || IEquals(cmd, "UNLOCK") || IEquals(cmd, "PUBLISH") || IEquals(cmd, "RESTORE") || ...` docs/spec/sys-views.md:194 `it is a verb the dispatcher routes, else `OTHER``

**Suggested.** Classify the first token once in DispatchInner into an enum (one table: verb, required role, writes-the-log, admitted-while-failed, admitted-read-only, statistics spelling), route by switch, and carry the kind on DispatchOutcome so RecordStatement does not re-tokenize. Until then, add the nine verbs to kKinds or correct sys-views.md; a test that walks the routing table against kKinds would pin it.

### H65. Every directory but base is one include cycle, and stats is the hub: 3.4 made it a dependency of sched, storage, txn and catalog while it still includes exec, parser and catalog

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence high); round 1, `arch-layering` (`arch-layering#1`)
- **Where**: `include/kds/stats/access_stats.hpp:12`; origin: pre-3.4 for stats -> exec (292df9b4, 2026-08-03) and wal -> stats (86d1fe1a, BO-S7); added by 3.4: sched -> stats (c5c2edc1, CJ), txn -> stats (c2925b6e, DF-S2), storage -> stats (29810582, DF-S4), catalog -> stats (6bedc13f, DI-S3), stats -> exec/plan_printer (89c26252, DA-S1)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** The directories are not layers. Computed over every '#include "kds/..."' line, each of sched, storage, wal, txn, catalog, stats, exec, parser, wire, bootstrap and server can reach every other one; only base is outside the cycle. There are 14 upward directory pairs carrying 32 include lines (exec -> parser excluded). The stats directory holds two unrelated kinds of unit: leaf counters that the lowest layers call (leaf_stats, txn_stats, wait_event, ash_ring, checkpoint_log, per_core_ring) and advisory structures built on exec and parser types (access_stats on exec::StepChain, cabin_store on parser::AstValue, trail_store on exec::TouchedTuple). Because both live in one directory and one library target, nothing stops a low layer from growing a real dependency on the high half, and four 3.4 milestones each added an inbound edge from a lower layer.

**Evidence.** Upward edges into stats: sched 3 lines (include/kds/sched/wait_state.hpp:13 stats/wait_event.hpp, sched/ash_sampler.hpp:11, src/sched/ash_sampler.cpp:3), storage 2 (src/storage/btree/btree.cpp:9 and src/storage/index/index_tree.cpp:7, 'stats::LeafStats::CountDivision(owner_oid);  // DF-S4'), wal 1 (src/wal/checkpointer.cpp:9), txn 1 (src/txn/manager.cpp:13), catalog 2 (include/kds/catalog/rows.hpp:14 stats/statement_binding.hpp). Edges out of stats: catalog 15 lines, storage 11, sched 9, exec 4 (access_stats.hpp:12 exec/step_chain.hpp; src/stats/access_stats.cpp:11 exec/plan_printer.hpp; trail_store.hpp:8 and trail_recorder.hpp:8 exec/trail_collector.hpp), parser 1 (cabin_store.hpp:16 parser/ast.hpp). src/stats/access_stats.cpp:11 pulls exec/plan_printer.hpp, whose closure is 63 headers / 20,737 lines (it includes exec/step_vm.hpp), for one function: 'const char* AccessKindName(AccessKind kind) noexcept;' (plan_printer.hpp:57). Transitive reach by TU: 8 of 9 catalog TUs reach stats and parser, 4 of 8 sched TUs reach stats, 3 of 23 storage TUs reach stats, 3 of 17 stats TUs reach exec. CMakeLists.txt:47 is the single 'add_library(kds' with 158 sources, so no target boundary enforces any direction. No file-level header cycle exists, so the split is mechanical.

**Suggested.** Split stats by direction: the counter half (per_core_ring, oid_parts, leaf_stats, txn_stats, wait_event, ash_record, ash_ring, checkpoint_log, statement_binding) depends on base and catalog/oid.hpp only and belongs below storage; the advisory half (cabin_store, waystone*, trail_*, plan_stats, access_stats, optimizer_signals, cabin_optimizer, managed_statistics) stays above exec. Move exec::AccessKind and AccessKindName into a small header so access_stats does not include step_vm.hpp. If the layering is meant to hold, give each layer its own library target (or a script in the push gate that fails on an upward include) so the next milestone cannot add a fifth edge unnoticed.

### H66. The instance's core ceiling and page 0's format live in server/, so txn includes the top layer and five lower-layer headers copy the literal 64, two of them tied to it by comment only

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence high); round 1, `arch-layering` (`arch-layering#2`)
- **Where**: `include/kds/server/superblock.hpp:413`; origin: pre-3.4 (txn -> server: 33086117 2026-08-04 and b5abab6a AN-S0/S1); the unasserted sched::kWaitCores copy came with CJ (c5c2edc1, 2026-10-31, 3.4)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** server/superblock.hpp is a storage-format header (its own includes are base and storage only) placed in the top layer. It is the direct include of two txn headers, catalog.cpp and bootstrap.hpp, and through them 46 of 162 src TUs and 164 of 289 test TUs compile it; 4 of 10 txn TUs transitively include a server header for it. It also owns kMaxCores, the size of every per-core array. Layers that will not include upward each restate the number: an implicit cross-file contract kept by comment, against the working rule that a quantity has one name. Four copies are checked by static_asserts that sit in server TUs; sched::kWaitCores and wal::Writer::kWaitSlots are checked by nothing, and the second is private so no outside assert can reach it.

**Evidence.** superblock.hpp:413 'inline constexpr std::uint32_t kMaxCores = 64;'. Upward users: include/kds/txn/trx_id.hpp:10 (holds 'server::SuperBlock& superblock_;' at :162), include/kds/txn/instance_visibility.hpp:13 (only use: 'std::array<CoreVisibilitySlot, server::kMaxCores> slots_{};' at :661, :667, :668), src/catalog/catalog.cpp:15 (server::kFirstUserPageId at :36), include/kds/bootstrap/bootstrap.hpp:8. Copies of 64: include/kds/base/contention.hpp:185 kContentionSlots, include/kds/sched/checkpoint_census.hpp:179 kSlots, include/kds/sched/core_load.hpp:40 kSlots, include/kds/sched/wait_state.hpp:226 'kWaitCores = 64;  // `kMaxCores` (superblock.hpp)', include/kds/wal/writer.hpp:195-198 '// `kMaxCores` (superblock.hpp, which this layer does not include)' then 'static constexpr std::uint32_t kWaitSlots = 64;'. Asserted: src/server/expeditor.cpp:895-897 (kContentionSlots, CheckpointCensus::kSlots, CoreLoad::kSlots) and src/server/sys_views.cpp:67 (stats::kStatsParts). Not asserted: kWaitCores and kWaitSlots (grep for a static_assert naming either: none). kWaitCores is used as a modulus, 'g_cores[core % kWaitCores]' (src/sched/wait_state.cpp:108, :114), so a mismatch would fold two cores' wait totals together rather than fail.

**Suggested.** Move kMaxCores to a base header (base/common.hpp or a new base/limits.hpp) and have the five copies name it; that removes the reason for the copies and the txn/instance_visibility.hpp include. Move superblock.hpp/.cpp under storage/ (its dependencies are already base and storage), which removes txn -> server, catalog -> server and bootstrap -> server in one step. Until then, add the two missing static_asserts (kWaitSlots needs a public alias or an assert inside writer.cpp against a base constant).

### H67. command_dispatcher.cpp grew 34% in 3.4 to 15,675 lines; its header's closure is 130 headers and 101 test TUs recompile on any change to it

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `arch-layering` (`arch-layering#3`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:11832`; origin: pre-3.4 structure; v3.4.0..HEAD added 5,475 and deleted 1,508 lines in the .cpp (11,708 -> 15,675) and 520/143 in the .hpp (2,779 -> 3,156), across CX, CY, DC, DF, DI and the others
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** One class in one translation unit carries every statement family: DML, DDL, plan DDL, statistics DDL, Waystone, purge, the locking read, the catalog views' entry and the sys.meta report. The file is 11% of src+include by lines and is the unit every 3.4 milestone edited. The header is not implementation-heavy (989 code lines, 1,923 comment lines of 3,156), but it directly includes 48 kds headers and transitively 130 (37,832 lines), and it is in the closure of 101 of 289 test TUs and 8 src TUs, so each milestone's edit rebuilds a third of the suite's objects. A seam already exists and is in use: src/server/sys_views.cpp defines 34 CommandDispatcher members in a second TU.

**Evidence.** Function-length scan of the .cpp (column-0 definitions to the closing brace): 249 functions, 13,726 lines inside them, 35 functions of 100 lines or more, 6 of 300 or more totalling 2,890 lines: UpdateParsed 747 lines at :11832, MetaSections 614 at :2140, InsertOneRow 501 at :8240, SelectBody 382 at :10151, HandleWaystone 323 at :14150, DispatchInner 323 at :1663. 'CommandDispatcher::' occurs 195 times in command_dispatcher.cpp, 34 in sys_views.cpp, 2 in kwp_load_server.cpp. The class head is at include/kds/server/command_dispatcher.hpp:520 with about 133 data-member lines and 33 setter/attach methods. Self-contained families still in the big file: MetaSections (:2140, a sys view body), HandleStatistics (:3754, 267 lines), HandleIndex (:4031), HandleWaystone (:14150), HandleCreatePlan (:14540), HandlePurge (:14990), HandlePurgeDroppedColumns (:15195). The function scan is a heuristic and may be off by a few functions; the named line numbers were checked with grep.

**Suggested.** Follow the sys_views.cpp precedent: move member definitions by statement family into their own TUs without changing the class (meta report beside sys_views.cpp; plan/statistics/Waystone/purge handlers; the INSERT family; the UPDATE/DELETE family). That is a move with no behaviour change and it lets parallel milestones stop colliding in one file. Separately, trim command_dispatcher.hpp's 48 includes with forward declarations where a member is a pointer or reference, measured by the closure count (130 headers today) and by a clean-build time of kds_tests in build-release.

### H68. storage and wal include each other; storage/ holds two layers, and storage/log_page_image.hpp is a WAL-logging helper no storage file uses

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence high); round 1, `arch-layering` (`arch-layering#4`)
- **Where**: `include/kds/storage/log_page_image.hpp:11`; origin: pre-3.4 (log_page_image.hpp added at 4eae38fd, 2026-08-19; the checkpoint targets and wal/durability.hpp includes are older than v3.4.0)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** storage -> wal is 8 include lines in 5 headers and wal -> storage is 13 lines over 10 storage headers, so neither directory can be built or reasoned about without the other. The cause is that storage/ contains both the page formats (which the log's redo must know, below wal) and the buffer pool with its checkpoint targets (which must know the log, above wal). log_page_image.hpp is the clearest misplacement: it includes four wal headers and is included by no file under storage; its five includers are exec (3), catalog (1) and server (1). wal/redo.cpp also reaches up into txn for the undo page format.

**Evidence.** storage -> wal: include/kds/storage/device_page_store.hpp:28 and storage/page_mgr/page_mgr.hpp:9 (wal/durability.hpp); storage/page_store_checkpoint_target.hpp:7 and storage/page_mgr/checkpoint_target.hpp:7 (wal/checkpointer.hpp); storage/log_page_image.hpp:11-14 ('#include "kds/wal/manager.hpp"', payload.hpp, record.hpp, stream.hpp; closure 22 headers / 5,816 lines). Includers of log_page_image.hpp: src/exec/assertion_build.cpp:12, src/exec/index_ddl.cpp:18, src/exec/wal_row_log.cpp:6, src/catalog/catalog.cpp:20, src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:71. wal -> storage headers: page_store.hpp (3), keystone.hpp (2), varheap.hpp, page_header.hpp, index/index_page.hpp, heap/heap_page.hpp, crc32c.hpp, cabin_bound_page.hpp, btree/btree.hpp, anchor_page.hpp. wal -> txn: src/wal/redo.cpp:19 txn/undo_page.hpp ('txn::UndoPageWriteAt(page, ...)' at :188).

**Suggested.** Name the two halves: page formats and devices (page_header, heap_page, btree_page, index_page, varheap, keystone, crc32c, page_device) below wal; the pool (device_page_store, page_mgr, the checkpoint targets, page_reader, data_syncer) above it. Move log_page_image.hpp to wal/ or exec/ beside wal_row_log, where its users are. No behaviour changes; the gain is that redo's dependency on page formats and the pool's dependency on the log stop reading as a cycle.

### H69. A committed DROP TABLE tells the memory stores through a hand-kept list written twice; each 3.4 milestone added a line at both sites and one store was missed

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence high); round 1, `arch-state-stores` (`arch-state-stores#1`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:4999`; origin: 3.4.*: CX-S1a (4f62dd2a, 4f12b44d), CY-S1b (61f5dddd), DI-S3 (6bedc13f) each added one line at both sites
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** There is no single seam that says 'this relation is gone' to the oid-keyed stores. The same four calls are written once on the no-transaction path (lines 4999-5002) and once on the commit path (lines 9165-9170), and Cabin sets are forgotten at a third place and a different time (line 5007, at the statement, before the commit). Of about thirteen oid-keyed stores, five are told (CapturedPlans, AdminPlans, ManagedStatistics, the Waystone opt-ins, CabinStore), one heals lazily (PlanStats priors, plan_stats.cpp:469) and the rest are never told. Most of the untold ones are recorded per store in known-gaps; PlanAdvices (include/kds/exec/plan_advice.hpp:52) is not: it has no ForgetRelation, so sys.plan_advice keeps a row naming a dropped relation until 1,024 newer shapes evict it or the instance restarts.

**As narrowed by verification.** A committed DROP TABLE reaches the oid-keyed memory stores through a hand-kept four-call list written twice (command_dispatcher.cpp:4999-5002 and :9165-9170), each line added by a different 3.4 commit at both sites, with Cabin sets forgotten at a third place and at statement time (:5006-5008). No single 'relation is gone' seam exists. PlanAdvices is not told, but this is the stated contract of sys.plan_advice (sys-views.md:114: 'a relation dropped since reads `oid <n>`'), not a missed store; no compile reads it, so the cost is a stale view row until eviction or restart. The finding is a maintainability one (two lists to keep in step), with no wrong answer shown.

**Evidence.** command_dispatcher.cpp:4999-5002: `captured_plans_->ForgetRelation(oid.value()); admin_plans_->ForgetRelation(oid.value()); ForgetRelationStatistics(oid.value()); EndOptInsNaming(oid.value());` and the same four at 9165-9170 on `owed->oid`. git blame gives each of the four lines a different commit. plan_advice.cpp:16-27 has Record and Snapshot only; sys_views.cpp:1073-1083 labels each key oid through RelationLabels for the view.

**Suggested.** One `ForgetRelation(oid)` on the dispatcher (or a small registry of drop listeners held by the instance) called from both sites, with every oid-keyed store either registered or carrying a stated 'not told, because' line. Add PlanAdvices to it.

### H70. The bounded keyed table is built about ten times with seven different full-table rules, two hand-written open-addressing tables and five copies of the hash finaliser

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence high); round 1, `arch-state-stores` (`arch-state-stores#2`)
- **Where**: `include/kds/stats/oid_parts.hpp:41`; origin: pre-3.4 for OidParts, TrailRecorder, CabinStore, PlanStats; 3.4.* added AccessStats' flat table (DA), CapturedPlans and PlanAdvices (CX), StatementStats' binding key and the signal key (DI)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** OidParts was written 'once so a store cannot bound, drop or merge differently from another', but only RelationStats, IndexStats and LeafStats use it. What a full table does differs by store: drop and count (OidParts::With, oid_parts.hpp:47; StatementStats, statement_stats.cpp:85, a hand copy of With's find / bound / try-emplace shape; AccessStats, access_stats.cpp:64; PlanStats::Fold, plan_stats.cpp:344), evict least recently seen (PlanStats master, plan_stats.cpp:422), evict coldest by a full scan (optimizer_signals.cpp:42), evict oldest by a full scan (plan_advice.cpp:18), clear the whole table (trail_recorder.cpp:17, cabin_store.cpp:214-224), refuse the new shape (captured_plans.cpp:61), two-way LRU (plan_memo.cpp:37). The flat half-load linear-probe table exists twice (AccessStats::Record and PlanStats::Fold), and the splitmix64 finaliser is written out in captured_plans.cpp:14, statement_stats.cpp:55, access_stats.cpp:20, optimizer_signals.cpp (SignalKeyHash) and plan_memo.cpp:8.

**As narrowed by verification.** OidParts ('written once so a store cannot bound, drop or merge differently') has three clients; about ten other bounded keyed tables each carry their own full-table rule (drop-and-count x4, evict-LRU, evict-coldest by scan, evict-oldest by scan, clear wholesale x2, refuse, 2-way LRU), each stated in its own file. Two flat half-load linear-probe tables are hand-written (AccessStats::Record, PlanStats::Fold), and StatementStats repeats OidParts::With's shape by hand over a non-oid key. splitmix64's finaliser is written out in full at four store sites (captured_plans.cpp:14, access_stats.cpp:22, optimizer_signals.cpp:47, plan_stats.cpp:21) and three more outside them (lock_table.cpp:18, memory_page_device.cpp:322, tcp_server.cpp:591); statement_stats.cpp:55 is a partial variant; plan_memo.cpp:8 is a different mixer, not a copy.

**Evidence.** oid_parts.hpp:13-15: 'the shape `RelationStats` and `IndexStats` share (BO-Q5 (b), BO-Q7 (a)), written once so a store cannot bound, drop or merge differently from another.' statement_stats.cpp:83-108 repeats the same find / `size() >= kShapesPerCore` / try-emplace / `++part.dropped` sequence by hand. access_stats.cpp:61-66 and plan_stats.cpp:339-346 are the same probe loop with the same half-load cut.

**Suggested.** Generalise OidParts to a key type (StatementStats becomes a client), factor the flat half-load table shared by AccessStats and PlanStats' parts, and put one Mix64 in base. State the full-table rule per store in one table in sys-views.md, so the differences are decisions and not accidents. DL's census (DL-S0) is the natural place for this.

### H71. A paced GATHER STATISTICS holds its core's statement epoch for the whole walk, so PURGE DROPPED COLUMNS and dropped-page reclaim anywhere in the instance wait it out

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: medium, confidence medium); round 1, `dispatcher-1` (`dispatcher-1#1`)
- **Where**: `src/server/command_dispatcher.cpp:3589`; origin: CY-S1b (61f5dddd), pace added by CY-Q31 (a)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** GatherRun is handed on through BuildRun::HandOn(fenced=true), which takes a SuspensionHold: this core's statement epoch at the head's schema word plus a catalog memo pin, kept until the run is destroyed. The gather is deliberately paced (16 leaves per >= 2 ms, in practice the 10 ms idle-block ceiling, about 1,600 leaves/s by its own comment, 'a million-leaf relation in some ten minutes'). For that whole time every gate that asks StatementEpochs::AllAtLeast(word) for a word moved after the gather's head answers false: PURGE DROPPED COLUMNS on any relation (15242) and the dropped-relation / index page reclaim (page_reclaim.cpp:141). The gather reads slot counts of one relation and no cell of any other, so the hold is far coarser than what it protects, and the pacing multiplies its length. known-gaps.md's gather entry (2401-2414) and plan-stats.md PS20 state the epoch hold as what keeps the leaf in place but not this consequence.

**Evidence.** 3589: `DispatchOutcome Start() { return HandOn(/*fenced=*/true); }`; 3128: `hold_.emplace(d_);` (SuspensionHold ctor 3014-3015: `word_ = d_.catalog_.cache_built_at(); d_.epochs_->Hold(d_.core_id_, word_);`); 3586: `pace_ns_ = kGatherSliceIntervalNs;`. Gate: 15241-15242 `const std::uint64_t word = catalog_.cache_built_at(); if (epochs_ != nullptr && !epochs_->AllAtLeast(word)) {` -> TxnConflict + purge_wait, which DispatchAsync (1055-1083) polls for kPurgeHorizonWaitNs = 1 s and then answers the refusal. statement_epoch.hpp:136-138 Republish publishes the lowest held word.

**Scenario.** Session A: GATHER STATISTICS ON big (1M leaves, runs ~10 min paced). Session B: ALTER TABLE t DROP COLUMN c (moves the schema word), then PURGE DROPPED COLUMNS t. B waits 1 s and is refused TXN_CONFLICT 'an older statement may still read its dropped columns as live' on every attempt until A's gather ends, although A never reads t. A DROP TABLE committed in that window likewise keeps its pages unreclaimed until the gather ends. Not a wrong answer: an admin statement refused, and space held, for the length of a walk that was slowed on purpose.

**Suggested.** Give the gather a hold per slice instead of per run: take the SuspensionHold (or just Enter/Publish the epoch) inside Step beside the per-slice IS borrow and release it at the yield, re-validating the resume leaf with the CheckOwned test RelationGather::Step already makes (plan_stats.cpp:765) and stopping when the store has forgotten the oid. If the per-run hold is kept, state the consequence in known-gaps.md's gather entry and plan-stats.md PS20.

### H72. The CV index-order elision is decided twice from stmt.order_by and joined only by the emit_order annotation

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence high); round 1, `exec-compiler` (`exec-compiler#2`)
- **Where**: `src/exec/step_compiler.cpp:3634`; origin: CV-S2 (10e7b0a4), CV-S3 (7d11ba44)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **confirmed**

**Claim (the finder's).** Whether an index holds the asked order is decided in section 4 from DriverOrderOf's own resolution of stmt.order_by (line 3247, used at 3463-3471), inside the 630-line CompileBound. Section 5a then resolves stmt.order_by a second time into chain.sort_keys (3571-3583) and line 3634 clears them whenever step 0 carries an index emit order, without comparing the keys it drops with the DriverOrder that justified the walk. The two agree today only because both read the same statement by the same rules; anything 5a later learns about a sort key that DriverOrderOf does not mirror (a null placement, an expression key, a key rewritten between the sections) turns directly into a dropped sort, which is a quiet-wrong order with no refusal. The ascending tuple key elision and O2 do not have this shape: they test chain.sort_keys themselves (3613-3627).

**Evidence.** 3247: `const std::optional<DriverOrder> driver_order = DriverOrderOf(scope, stmt);` 3634-3636: `if (!chain.steps.empty() && IsIndexOrder(chain.steps[0].emit_order)) { chain.sort_keys.clear(); }` 1363: DriverOrderOf calls `ResolveColumn(scope, written.key.column)`; 3575 calls it again for the same keys.

**Scenario.** None at HEAD (the two resolutions agree). The risk is a future change to one side only.

**Suggested.** Build chain.sort_keys before section 4 (it needs only the scope and stmt.order_by) and have DriverOrderOf read the resolved SortKeys; at the elision, assert or check that the sort keys are exactly the step's index->key_cols after eq_prefix, in one direction, with at most a trailing slot-0 tuple key, before clearing. That also removes the second ResolveColumn pass per ORDER BY key per compile (per permutation under the join search). Section 4's per-step ladder (3248-3504) reads only scope, step, slot, pin, driver_order and inner_build and can leave CompileBound as one function at the same time.

### H73. AcceptTupleAt takes its per-row context through runner members, and 3.4 added three more such channels

- **Kind**: architecture; severity low (the finder's: low, confidence medium); round 1, `exec-vm` (`exec-vm#5`)
- **Where**: `src/exec/step_vm.cpp:3397`; origin: pre-3.4 pattern (recording_, building_, memo); extended by CU-S1..S3 (key_set_leaf_) and CV-S2/S3 (ordered_key_, ordered_matched_, ordered_collect_)
- **Verification**: first verifier: **narrowed**

**Claim (the finder's).** AcceptTupleAt (2741-3170, about 430 lines) is the single visibility-and-filter site for every access kind, but what a caller means by a given call is not in its signature. It is read from ChainRunner members the caller sets just before and clears just after: key_set_leaf_ (3282), ordered_key_ / ordered_matched_ / ordered_collect_ (3397-3401), recording_ / completing_recording_ (3314, 3338) and building_ (3358). Because AcceptTupleAt re-enters RunStep for deeper steps on the same runner, each channel is safe only by a re-entrancy argument written in a comment, and the same hazard has already had to be fixed by hand twice for scratch vectors.

**As narrowed by verification.** The structural fact holds. AcceptTupleAt (src/exec/step_vm.cpp:2741 to about 3170) takes per-call context through ChainRunner members rather than its signature, and 3.4 added key_set_leaf_ (CU-S1, 2066487c) and ordered_key_ / ordered_matched_ / ordered_collect_ (CV-S2, 10e7b0a4) beside the older recording_, completing_recording_ and building_.
